# OF PHYSICIANS
## AND PHILOSOPHERS, THE UNDISPUTED LEADER
->The five books of Theophrastus Paracelsus <term>the Hermit</term> <note>original: "Eremitæ." This refers to his family name, von Hohenheim, but also suggests his persona as a solitary seeker of wisdom.</note><-

->filled with unknown things,<-
->and until now, treated<-
->by no one,<-

->of Valentius de Retiis, and the *dedicatory*<-

->*excellent learning is praised.*<-

->Bear and forbear. <note>original Greek: "Ανέχου καὶ ἀπέχου" (Anechou kai apechou). A famous Stoic maxim attributed to Epictetus, advising endurance of hardship and restraint from vice.</note><-

--- Page 7 ---
<meta>This page is the dedication of a work by Adam von Bodenstein, a key figure in the spread of Paracelsian medicine. It is addressed to Ludwig Wolfgang von Habsberg, an official for the Margrave of Baden.</meta>

# TO THE MAN MOST DISTINGUISHED BY BIRTH, LEARNING,
## AND PRUDENCE,

counselor to the most Illustrious Prince Charles, Margrave of
Baden and Hachberg, etc.: and Governor in Ba-
denweiler, Ludwig Wolfgang von Habsberg,
a Lord and Brother to be respected,
->Adam von Bodenstein,<-
->most devoted to Theophrastus,<-
->prays for a prosperous and long life.<-

---

<image-desc>A large decorative woodcut initial 'N' featuring an intricate design of scrolling vines and foliage within a square frame.</image-desc>

NOBLE NATURE,
most excellent sir,
clearly teaches us that
the public good must always be
placed before private interests: for JEHOVAH <note>The Hebrew name for God, commonly used by Renaissance scholars to emphasize a return to biblical sources.</note> 
does good to all creatures at once: He
shares the Sun with both the good and the bad, and 
commands us to seek the welfare of others just as 
much as our own. Furthermore, right reason <term>recta ratio: a philosophical concept referring to the innate human ability to discern moral truths and natural laws</term> 
persuades us that benefits which are widely accessible, 
vital and significant to many, should be preferred over 
those that are narrow, personal, and minor, because 
the welfare of the whole [is better]...

<summary>This page contains a formal dedication by Adam von Bodenstein to Ludwig Wolfgang von Habsberg, an advisor to the Margrave of Baden. Bodenstein uses the dedication to argue that the public good and the sharing of knowledge should take precedence over private interests, citing both divine and rational precedents.</summary>

<keywords>Adam von Bodenstein, Ludwig Wolfgang von Habsberg, Charles Margrave of Baden, Badenweiler, Theophrastus Paracelsus, public good, right reason, Renaissance dedication, philosophy of science</keywords>

# TO THE MAN MOST DISTINGUISHED IN LINEAGE, 
## LEARNING, AND WISDOM,

-> to the Counselor of the most Illustrious Prince Charles, Marquis in <-
-> Baden and Hachberg, etc.: And Governor in <-
-> Badenweiler, Ludwig Wolfgang von Happfberg, <-
-> his respected Lord and Brother, Adam <-
-> von Bodenstein, most devoted to <term>Theophrastus</term> <note>Theophrastus Bombastus von Hohenheim, better known as Paracelsus (1493–1541), the famous physician and alchemist whose works Bodenstein edited.</note>, <-
-> prays for a prosperous and long life. <-

***

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut initial letter 'N' featuring a detailed floral or architectural scene inside the frame of the letter.</image-desc> MORE EXCELLENT NATURE, most eminent man, clearly teaches us that public goods should always be prioritized over private ones. For JEHOVAH <note>The Latin *IEHOVA*, a name for God. The author uses this to emphasize that divine providence is universal.</note> grants benefits to all creatures simultaneously: He shares the Sun with both the good and the bad, and He commands us to seek the well-being of others just as we seek our own. Furthermore, right reason suggests that benefits which are widely accessible—being more vital and significant to many—should be favored over those that are narrow, personal, and minor, because the whole is rather...

<summary>This page contains a formal dedication by Adam von Bodenstein to Ludwig Wolfgang von Happfberg, framing the publication of Paracelsus's medical or alchemical knowledge as an act of public service. Bodenstein argues that sharing knowledge for the common good is a divine and rational duty.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Adam von Bodenstein’s dedication to Ludwig Wolfgang von Habsberg. On the previous page, Bodenstein argued that the public good must be prioritized over private interests, citing divine example. Here, he shifts to classical philosophy to reinforce the idea that humans are naturally social beings meant to help one another.</meta>

->E P I S T L E<-

...greater care must be taken than for just a single part. Hence, it was excellently written by Plato (as Cicero notes <note>original: "ait Cicero". Bodenstein is quoting Marcus Tullius Cicero’s "De Officiis" (On Duties), 1.22, where Cicero himself quotes Plato’s "Ninth Letter".</note>): We are not born for ourselves alone, but our country claims a share of our birth, and our friends a share; and (as it pleases the Stoics <note>A school of Hellenistic philosophy that emphasized living in accordance with nature and the brotherhood of all humanity.</note>) all things produced on earth are created for the use of humankind, **BUT HUMANKIND WAS GENERATED FOR THE SAKE OF HUMANKIND**, so that they might be able to benefit one another. In this, we ought to follow nature as our guide, and bring common benefits into the public sphere through the exchange of duties <term>officia: the moral obligations or "services" one owes to others in a community</term>, by giving and receiving—binding together the society of men among men by our skills, our labor, and our resources. For things that are weak and poorly joined are quickly dissolved and perish; but stability and immortality are joined to harmony and the constant, firm coherence of the parts, because

<meta>catchword: præ- [prefixing the next page's first word, likely "præstat"]</meta>

<summary>Bodenstein quotes Cicero and Plato to argue that humans are social creatures born to serve their country and each other, emphasizing that a stable society is built on the mutual exchange of skills and resources.</summary>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page continues Adam von Bodenstein’s dedication to Ludwig Wolfgang von Habsberg. Having previously established the Stoic and Platonic principle that humans are born to serve one another, Bodenstein now introduces Theophrastus Paracelsus as the ultimate example of a man who dedicated his divine gifts to the service of humanity and the preservation of life.</meta>

### H E  C O M M E N D S

The most excellent doctor **Theophrastus Paracelsus**, with **divine light** <note>original: "lumine diuino"</note> shining before him in wondrous ways, always kept this in mind and observed it: he did not bury the **talent** <term>talent: a reference to the Parable of the Talents in Matthew 25; here it refers to Paracelsus’s natural and spiritual gifts which he was obligated to use for the benefit of others</term> entrusted to him by the Lord in the earth like a useless servant. Instead, with the greatest diligence, as befits a virtuous man, he put it out to interest and won back for the Lord countless people. 

He not only preserved these people so they would not be handed over to savage **death** <note>original: "orco" (Orcus), the Roman god of the underworld or the underworld itself.</note>, but also—by a certain more **hidden medicine** granted to him from heaven for this purpose—he made both their bodies and their souls stronger, more agile, and purer, transforming them, as it were, into a **divine nature**. 

He offered his **Art** to everyone, though few accepted it because the time was not yet right, just as his own writings testify. Now, however, through many and various **books**, most clearly...

<sig>a 3</sig>

<summary>Bodenstein introduces Paracelsus as a divinely inspired physician who, unlike the "useless servant" of the Bible, used his secret medical knowledge to heal both the bodies and souls of many, even though his work was not yet fully appreciated in his own time.</summary>

<keywords>Theophrastus Paracelsus, divine light, talent, hidden medicine, divine nature, healing of body and soul</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues the introductory dedication, providing the philosophical and ethical justification for the physician’s work. Having previously introduced Paracelsus as a man who used his "talents" for others, Bodenstein now anchors this behavior in the classical tradition of Plato, Cicero, and Stoic philosophy, emphasizing that humans exist to serve the common good.</meta>

# E P I S T L E

...care must be taken [for the whole] rather than just for one part. From this, it was excellently written by **Plato** (as **Cicero** says): **“We are not born for ourselves alone, but our country claims a part of our birth, and our friends a part.”** And, as the **Stoics** <term>Stoics: a school of Hellenistic philosophy that taught that humans are part of a single community and have a moral duty to help others and follow the laws of nature</term> would have it: all things produced on the earth are created for the use of humans, but **HUMANS WERE BORN FOR THE SAKE OF OTHER HUMANS**, so that they might be able to help one another.

In this, we ought to follow **Nature** as our guide, and bring common benefits into the open through an **exchange of duties** <note>original: "mutatione officiorum"</note>—by both giving and receiving—and thus bind together the **society** <note>original: "societatem"</note> of humans among themselves through our arts, our labor, and our resources. 

For things that are weak and poorly held together are quickly dissolved and perish; but stability and immortality are joined to **harmony** <note>original: "concordiæ"</note> and the constant and firm coherence of the parts, because...

<meta>catchword: præ-</meta>

<note>Vocabulary: Plato, Cicero, Stoics, homeland, society, harmony, duty</note>

<summary>Bodenstein argues that humans are naturally social beings with a duty to serve the common good, quoting Plato and Cicero to support the idea that we are born to help one another through our specific talents and resources.</summary>

<keywords>Plato, Cicero, Stoicism, social duty, common good, harmony, nature, mutual aid</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from general philosophical duties to the specific figure of Paracelsus. # C O M M E N D A T I O N

The most excellent **Doctor Theophrastus Paracelsus**, with **divine light** shining before him in wondrous ways, always kept in mind and observed his duty. He did not bury the **talent** <note>original: "talentum" — a reference to the biblical Parable of the Talents (Matthew 25), where a servant is judged for how he invests the resources his master entrusted to him</note> entrusted to him by the Lord in the earth like a useless servant. Instead, with the greatest diligence, as befits a virtuous man, he put it to work to earn interest, and thus won back for the Lord countless people. 

These were people whom he not only preserved so they would not be handed over to **cruel death** <note>original: "sæuo orco" — Orcus was a Roman god of the underworld; the author uses this classical image to represent the grave or the afterlife</note>, but through a certain **more secret medicine** <term>secret medicine: likely referring to Paracelsus’s "Spagyric" or alchemical medicines, which he claimed were revealed by God and were more effective than the "Galenic" herbal medicine of the time</term> given to him from heaven for this purpose, he made both their **bodies** and their **souls** stronger, more agile, and purer. Indeed, he transformed them, as it were, into a divine nature. 

He offered his **Art** to everyone, though few accepted it because the time was not yet right, as his own writings testify. But now, in many and various books, most clearly...

<sig>a 3</sig>

<note>Vocabulary: Theophrastus Paracelsus, medicine, soul, body, divine, talent</note>

<summary>Bodenstein praises Paracelsus as a divinely inspired physician who did not hide his God-given skills but used them to heal both the bodies and souls of countless people. He argues that while Paracelsus's "Art" was once rejected because the world was not ready, it is now being revealed through his many books.</summary>

<keywords>Paracelsus, divine inspiration, Parable of the Talents, spiritual healing, medical art, alchemy, soul and body</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical justification for the physician’s work, moving from the specific example of Paracelsus’s "talent" to the broader classical and Stoic principles of social duty. It emphasizes that individuals exist to serve the collective good, a principle the author uses to frame the publication of these medical texts.</meta>

->EPISTLE<-

<meta>catchword: prae-</meta>

...greater care must be taken than of just a single part. Wherefore it was most nobly written by **Plato** (as **Cicero** recounts): *We are not born for ourselves alone, but our country claims a share of our birth, and our friends a share.* <note>original: "Non nobis solùm nati sumus..." — a famous quote from Cicero’s *On Duties* (De Officiis), which he attributes to Plato’s Seventh Letter. It was a foundational text for Renaissance ethics.</note> 

And (as the **Stoics** <term>Stoics: a school of ancient Greek philosophy that taught that humans are part of a single social body and should live in accordance with nature and reason</term> believe) all things produced on earth were created for the use of humanity, **BUT HUMAN BEINGS WERE BORN FOR THE SAKE OF OTHER HUMAN BEINGS**, so that they might benefit one another. 

In this, we ought to follow nature as our guide, and bring the common good into the center by an exchange of **duties** <note>original: "officiorum" — referring to the moral and social obligations one has toward others</note>, by both giving and receiving; and thus, through our skills, our labor, and our resources, we may bind together the **human society** that exists among us. For things that are weak and poorly joined are quickly dissolved and perish; but stability and immortality are linked to **harmony** <note>original: "concordiæ" — the idea of "concord" or hearts beating together</note> and to the constant, firm coherence of the parts, because...

<summary>This page cites classical authorities like Plato, Cicero, and the Stoics to argue that humans are naturally social beings born to serve the common good. It frames the physician’s work and the sharing of knowledge as a fundamental duty to hold society together.</summary>

<keywords>Cicero, Plato, Stoics, social duty, common good, harmony, human society, ethics, philosophy</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from the general Stoic duty of serving others to the specific example of Paracelsus. ->HE COMMENDS<-

The most excellent doctor, **Theophrastus Paracelsus**, with the divine light shining before him in wonderful ways, always turned over in his mind and observed this: that he should not, like a useless servant, bury in the earth the **talent** <note>original: "talentum" — an allusion to the Biblical Parable of the Talents (Matthew 25), where a servant is expected to invest and grow the resources given to him by his master. Paracelsus is presented as the "good servant" who increased his gift.</note> handed to him by the Lord. Instead, with the greatest diligence, as befits a worthy man, he put it to work for interest, and thus gained for the Lord innumerable people. 

These people he not only preserved, so that they might not be handed over to cruel **Orcus** <term>Orcus: a classical name for the underworld or the god of death; here used metaphorically to mean a premature or agonizing death</term>, but also, by a certain more hidden medicine granted to him from on high for this purpose, he made both their bodies and their souls stronger, more agile, and purer, transforming them into something almost like a divine nature. 

He offered his art to everyone, though few received it because the time was not yet right, just as his own writings testify. But now, most clearly in many and various books...

->a 3<-

<summary>The author praises Paracelsus for using his God-given medical "talent" to save lives and purify souls, comparing his work to a divine mission that was often unappreciated in his own time.</summary>
<keywords>Paracelsus, Parable of the Talents, Orcus, divine light, spiritual healing, medical ethics, alchemy of the soul</keywords>

### EPISTOLA

[...especially] care must be held, rather than for only a single part. <note>The author is finishing a sentence begun on the previous page, likely paraphrasing Cicero’s advice that those who govern should care for the whole body of the state rather than favoring one faction.</note> For this reason, it was excellently written by **Plato** (as **Cicero** says): *We are not born for ourselves alone, but our country claims a share of our birth, and our friends another share;* <note>original: "Non nobis solùm nati sumus..." — a famous maxim from Cicero’s *De Officiis* (On Duties) 1.22, which he attributes to Plato's Epistles.</note> and (as it pleases the **Stoics** <term>Stoics: a school of ancient philosophy that taught that all humans belong to a single universal community and should labor for the benefit of all.</term>) *all things produced on the earth are created for the use of men, but* **MEN WERE GENERATED FOR THE SAKE OF MEN**, *so that they might be able to be useful to one another.* In this we ought to follow nature as our leader, and bring common interests into the center by an exchange of duties—by giving and receiving—and thus, through our skills, our labor, and our resources, bind together the society of men among men. For things that are weak and poorly joined are quickly dissolved and perish; but stability and immortality are joined to harmony and to a constant and firm cohesion of parts, because...

<summary>Citing Plato, Cicero, and Stoic philosophy, this page argues that humans are born for the sake of one another and must use their talents to strengthen the "bond of society." It concludes that the survival and "immortality" of a community depend on the harmonious cooperation and mutual service of its members.</summary>

->C O M M E N D A T I O N<-

<sig>a 3</sig>

...[because] the most excellent doctor **Theophrastus Paracelsus**, with divine light shining before him in wondrous ways, always intended and took care in his soul that he should not bury the talent <term>talent: an allusion to the Parable of the Talents in the Gospel of Matthew, where a servant is praised for investing his master's money rather than hiding it in the ground; here it refers to Paracelsus’s God-given intellectual and medical gifts.</term> entrusted to him by the Lord in the earth like a useless servant. Instead, with the greatest diligence, as befits a virtuous man, he put it out to interest and won back for the Lord countless men. These people he not only preserved, so that they might not be handed over to savage **Orcus** <term>Orcus: a name from Roman mythology for the underworld or the god of death; the author uses it here to represent a cruel or premature death.</term>, but also through a certain more secret medicine—granted to him from heaven for this very purpose—he rendered both their bodies and their souls stronger, more agile, and purer, transforming them into something almost like a divine nature. He offered his **Art** <note>original: "Artem suam" — Paracelsus often referred to his system of chemical medicine and hermetic philosophy as "The Art."</note> to all people, though few accepted it because the time was not yet right, just as his own writings testify. But now, through many and various books, it is revealed most clearly...

<summary>This page introduces Paracelsus as a divinely inspired figure who fulfilled his duty to humanity by sharing his "secret medicine" to save both bodies and souls. # LETTER

explained, it comes forth into the light. Although 
some who labor in dung, and like roosters 
and pigs <note>original: "galli suésq;" — A common polemical metaphor in Renaissance writing, comparing critics of new ideas to animals that prefer filth to gold or pearls.</note> rejoicing in it, will attempt to 
hinder and suppress it, nevertheless 
their efforts will be frustrated 
and vain, except among those in 
whom sin or confusion—which 
hates the light—too much predominates: 
for eternal light wills that the 
medicine of the soul and body <note>This reflects the Paracelsian ideal that true medicine is not just a physical remedy, but a spiritual one that aligns the patient with divine grace.</note> be 
offered to all men, so that help 
may be given to all, lest sin prevail 
over grace. To accomplish this, I also 
have been stirred up, I who at this time 
see no matter by which I might better 
promote the public good than if I 
share the books of **Theophrastus**, 
those vast treasures, with everyone: 
I will express in deeds the Art, which 
I know to be most certain and most true, 
and handed down by God himself for the 
use of men...

<summary>The author explains that Paracelsus's works are finally being published to provide universal healing, despite the efforts of critics to suppress them. # EPISTLE #

[...he de]scribed their properties: namely, that they exist before all things, that all things arise from them, and that they do not arise from one another—properties which Theophrastus <note>Paracelsus</note> confirms to be true. What, then, is the difference between them? I will explain as briefly as I can. Aristotle says the principles are **matter**, **form**, and **privation** <note>original: "materia, forma & privatio" — In Aristotelian physics, "privation" is the absence of a quality or form that a piece of matter is capable of having; change occurs when matter moves from privation to a specific form.</note>. Theophrastus, however, says they are **sulfur**, **Mercury**, and **salt** <note>These are the *Tria Prima* (Three Primes), which Paracelsus believed were the fundamental components of all physical substance.</note>.

For Aristotle says that it is necessary for there to be some one thing that is the **matter** of all things—the underlying subject and support <note>original: "fulcrum"</note>—from which all things first arise and into which they are ultimately resolved, and within which the **transmutations** <note>original: "transmutationes" — the changing of one substance into another.</note> of simple bodies take place. Next, he claims that two opposites must reside in matter; for no thing seeks its own destruction, nor does it harm itself insofar as it exists as a single entity. Thus, it is most clearly evident that transmutations occur from one opposite to another...

### EPISTLE

<note>hominum-</note> [benefit] <note>Completing the phrase "hominum commodo" from the previous page, meaning "for the benefit of men."</note> can always be shown in any body, as well as which [of the three principles] prevails. This is to be understood by <term>geometric proportion</term> <note>original: "geometrica proportione" — a relationship based on ratios and scaling rather than simple addition ("arithmetica"), common in early chemistry to describe how substances combine.</note>, not arithmetic, in mixed bodies, as the heat of fire—gathering together things of the same kind <note>original: "homogenea"</note> and dispersing things of different kinds <note>original: "heterogenea"</note>—most beautifully proves. 

Furthermore, <term>Form</term>, <term>Matter</term>, and <term>Privation</term> <note>These are the three "principles of nature" according to Aristotle. "Privation" refers to the state of a thing before it acquires a specific form.</note>, as the theoretical principles of things, appear nowhere—not even to the most subtle philosopher. Nonetheless, some insist that these are the true principles of nature, which she acts upon and creates in reality and in actual bodies, rather than just through imagination or the operation of the mind; otherwise, the principles of <term>Plato</term> and <term>Pythagoras</term> <note>Likely referring to Platonic "Ideas" or Pythagorean "Numbers" as the fundamental basis of reality.</note> would be far superior to those of **Aristotle**.

<meta>This page continues the author’s critique of Aristotelian natural philosophy. While the previous page argued that the three alchemical principles (Sulfur, Mercury, and Salt) are physically observable, this page contrasts them with Aristotle's abstract "Three Principles of Nature" (Matter, Form, and Privation), arguing that these abstractions cannot be perceived by the senses and are therefore less certain than physical bodies.</meta>

<header>COMMENDATION</header>

For nature begets **BODIES FROM BODIES** <note>original: "CORPORA EX CORPORIBVS" — a core materialist and alchemical argument that physical matter must originate from physical matter, rather than abstract ideas.</note>, and for that reason she recognizes no principles other than bodies. Secondly, all principles—from which academic disciplines are demonstrated and things arise—must be more prior, more familiar, simpler, and more certain than the effect itself. This is something that **Aristotle** himself and many philosophers admit with one voice. Yet those three [principles] established by the imagination <note>Referring to the Aristotelian triad of Matter, Form, and Privation mentioned on the previous page.</note> are neither more familiar nor more certain than the composite things they form, because neither reason nor the senses can grasp them. 

For things that have been brought into being are perceived by the senses: and reason gathers through the senses that they exist in a specific state—just as I see, touch, smell, and hear **Socrates**. Then, reason concludes that he is no other man than that specific individual, and indicates that he is not a <term>brute</term> <note>original: "brutum" — in early modern philosophy, this refers to an animal, which was believed to possess a soul and senses but lacked human reason.</note>, much less a plant, a metal, or any other kind of body. However, as for <term>Matter</term>, <term>Form</term>, and <term>Privation</term>...

principles, neither reason nor the senses can grasp them, even if you were to divide **Socrates** into a thousand parts, and then arrange and divide those parts according to the whim of your mind. 

**Aristotle** says that analogy or <term>proportion</term> <note>original: "proportio" — here meaning the logical relationship between things, rather than mathematical ratio.</note> teaches us these principles. For we observe in all composite things that there is a certain <term>matter</term> <note>original: "materiam" — in Aristotelian philosophy, "Prime Matter" is the underlying "stuff" of the universe that has no qualities of its own until it receives a "form."</note>, which exists as the subject of opposites during a change; and finally, through ultimate analysis, we conclude that there is only one subject common to all things. <meta>This page continues the author’s critique of Aristotelian physics. On the previous page, the author argued that the "principles" of nature (Matter, Form, and Privation) are logical abstractions rather than observable realities. This page specifically addresses "accidents"—the sensible qualities of things like color or smell—and argues that if the Aristotelian principles were true, they could not account for these qualities.</meta>

->C O M M E N D A T.<-

...all <term>accidents</term> <note>original: "accidentia" — in Aristotelian philosophy, these are non-essential properties like color, size, or smell that can change without the object itself changing its nature.</note> which are inherent in natural bodies and are presented to the senses also have their own proper principles. For why should <term>substance</term> <note>original: "substantia" — the underlying reality or "thingness" of an object.</note> possess its own principles more so than colors, sounds, flavors, smells, lights, and countless other things of this kind? 

Truly, they do not proceed from formless and void <term>matter</term> <note>original: "materia" — here referring to Prime Matter, which Aristotle described as having no qualities of its own.</note>, because such matter lacks every accident; otherwise, it would signify something definite. Nor do they proceed from <term>form</term> <note>original: "forma" — the organizing principle that makes a thing what it is.</note>, which is necessarily either substance or accident. If it is <term>essence</term> <note>original: "essentia"</note> (as most philosophers claim), accidents do not arise from it because essence is most simple and incorporeal, just as no substance is ever created from accidents. 

But if you were to say that form is an accident, then nothing certain can be signified, as it would be transcendent and belong to no specific <term>category</term> <note>original: "categoriae" — referring to Aristotle's classification system for all things in existence.</note>. For it cannot be <term>quality</term> <note>original: "qualitas"</note> alone, because from quality and the substance of every body...

**b** <note>This "b" is a signature mark, used by early printers to help bookbinders assemble the pages in the correct order.</note>

<summary>The author challenges the Aristotelian view of nature by arguing that abstract principles like "Matter" and "Form" cannot explain the origin of sensible qualities like color and sound. He asserts that if "Form" is simple and incorporeal, it cannot logically produce the physical properties we perceive.</summary>

--- Page 34 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s critique of Aristotelian physics and logic. While the previous page argued that "accidents" (sensible qualities) cannot arise from formless matter or incorporeal essence, this page extends that argument to other Aristotelian categories like quantity and quality. The author asserts that because these principles are logically flawed, they lead the human mind into a philosophical dead end.</meta>

# EPISTLE

...quantity cannot be produced skillfully, just as **quality** <note>original: "qualitas" — the characteristics or properties of a thing, like its color or texture.</note> arises neither from **quantity** <note>original: "quantitas" — the measurable aspect of a thing, like size or number.</note> nor from a **substance** of this kind. The same reasoning also applies to the remaining **categories** <note>original: "categorijs" — the ten classes into which Aristotle sorted all existing things, such as place, time, and posture.</note>. From **privation** <note>original: "privatio" — in Aristotelian physics, this refers to the absence of a quality that should be present. The author argues that since it is defined as a "nothing," it cannot be a building block of nature.</note>, which (by Aristotle’s own testimony) is nothing and does not exist in itself, much less can any natural thing be made. 

How then shall our reason know principles of this kind? Indeed, a false proportion is prescribed, which seduces reason while it searches anxiously, and points to a foundation beyond the limits of nature that can [hardly] ever be investigated. For the more diligently reason examines individual things and inquires into their principles, the more it wanders astray in its reasoning, and it perceives the foundation of the whole philosophy to be fragile, inconsistent, and fictitious. Because in inquiring into each thing in order, reason is miserably deceived in this way; yet Aristotle [offered] no more suitable account...

->with<-

<summary>The author argues that Aristotle's categories and the principle of 'privation' are logically impossible foundations for the natural world. He concludes that the more one studies this traditional philosophy, the more one realizes it is a fragile and deceptive fiction.</summary>

If you say that prime matter is corporeal, it immediately follows—contrary to the Aristotelians' own views—that the **elements** are not the simplest bodies, unless you wish to assert along with **Empedocles** <note>A Greek philosopher who famously proposed that all matter is composed of four "roots": Earth, Air, Fire, and Water.</note> that the prime matter of all things is the four elements. to have been born. Why, I pray, does an **incorporeal substance** <term>Latin: *substantia incorporea* — a reality that exists without having a physical body or mass</term> produce a **body** that is extremely light, thin, hot, and dry—which you call the **element of fire**—rather than one that is moist, cold, and heavy—which you call **water**? If such a distinction was made in nature, or is still being made today, some cause must necessarily exist for it. 

If you wish this to happen by reason of its **form** <term>Latin: *forma* — in ancient philosophy, the "essence" or "blueprint" that gives matter its specific identity</term>, then that form itself must have already been something of this kind beforehand. It must have contained, and still contain, the distinct natures of the four **simple bodies** <note>The four classical elements: Earth, Water, Air, and Fire</note>. If that is the case, it would not be an incorporeal substance, nor an **accident** <term>Latin: *accidens* — a quality that can change without destroying the thing itself, like being "hot" or "cold"</term>, but a physical body.

->C O M M E N D A T.<-

...are new; and nevertheless, the former do not precede the latter, since there is one and the same principle: **form** <term>Latin: *forma* — the "essence" or "blueprint" that gives matter its specific identity</term> succeeds **privation** <term>Latin: *privatio* — the state of lacking a form that should naturally be there</term>, and privation succeeds form. But it is asked: from where do they emerge again, and where do they go? 

If this form is a **substance** <term>Latin: *substantia* — a reality that exists in itself</term>, it must be that the substance perishes, unless it passes into **matter** <term>Latin: *materia* — the underlying "stuff" of the universe</term>. Yet they <note>Likely referring to the Scholastic followers of Aristotle</note> do not want this, because forms are not made from other things, nor from each other, even though substance receives **accidents** <term>Latin: *accidens* — a quality that can change without destroying the thing itself, like being "hot" or "cold"</term>. Indeed, every substance receives accidents, except for **God**. 

**Aristotle** <note>The Greek philosopher whose system of "hylomorphism" (matter and form) is being debated here</note> himself asserts that privation perishes; he seems to ascribe immortality to matter alone. For he says at the end of the first book of the ***Physics*** <note>Aristotle's foundational text on the study of nature and change</note>, regarding matter (which he often calls "privation" and "potentiality"): 

*[Matter] perishes and comes to be in one sense, but in another sense it does not. For as the thing in which [the privation exists], it perishes in its own right—for that which perishes in this is the privation. But as it exists according to its potential, it does not perish in its own right; rather, it must be incorruptible and ungenerated.* <note>original Greek: φθείρεται δ’ ἔτι καὶ γίνεται ἔστὶ μὲν ὡς, ἔστὶ δ’ ὡς ὄυ, ὡς μὲν γὰρ τὸ ἐν ὧ, κατ’ αὐτὸ φθείρεται, τὸ γὰρ φθειρόμενον ἐν τούτῳ ἐστὶν ἡ στέρησις, ὡς δὲ κατὰ δύναμιν, οὐ κατ’ αὐτὸ, ἀλλ’ ἄφθαρτον, καὶ ἀγέννητον ἀνάγκη αὐτὴν εἶναι.</note>

# EPISTLE

<meta>catchword: simul (likely "simultaneously")</meta>

This opinion regarding principles <term>Latin: *principia* — the foundational causes or origins of natural things</term> certainly confuses and undermines itself by **Aristotle’s** own words. Yet we see him teach that the "first form" is not a **substance**—as many interpreters claim—but rather the **primary qualities** <note>In Aristotelian physics, these are the four basic tactile qualities: hot, cold, moist, and dry.</note>. For in the first book of the ***Physics***, he states that **matter** is, in a sense, a substance, that **form** belongs to "primary wisdom" <note>Aristotle refers to "First Philosophy" or Metaphysics as "primary wisdom," the study of being as being.</note>, and that two contrary principles reside within matter. 

Later, in the first chapter of the second book of ***On Generation and Corruption*** <note>Original Latin title: *De Ortu ac interitu*; a work concerning how things are born and how they decay.</note>, explaining his view more clearly, he says: 

*But we say that there is a certain matter for bodies perceived by the senses, and that it is not separable, but always joined to a state of opposition, from which the elements arise.* 

And a little later he concludes: 

*Therefore, first indeed, the sensible body is a principle; second, the contraries heat and cold; thirdly, if—* <note>The text breaks off here at the end of the page; the catchword "mul" indicates the next word is *simul*, meaning "simultaneously" or "at once."</note>

<summary>The author uses Aristotle's own texts, specifically the Physics and On Generation and Corruption, to argue that form should be understood as physical qualities (like heat) rather than an abstract substance. This challenges the standard Scholastic interpretation of hylomorphism.</summary>

—taneously <note>Completing the word *simultaneously* from the previous page's catchword.</note> water and fire, and others of the same kind. He calls **matter** a body that is "sensible in potential" <term>Latin: *potentia sensile* — something that has the capacity to be perceived by the senses once it takes on a form, though it has no qualities of its own yet</term>: because it is the true essence, and he intended for physical bodies to be "born" or generated from it. He defines **Form** as the opposites <term>Latin: *contraria* — the primary qualities of hot, cold, moist, and dry</term> that exist within matter, from which the "simple bodies" <note>The four basic elements: earth, air, fire, and water.</note> are created in the third step of the process.

**Galen** <note>Claudius Galenus (129–c. 216 AD), the most influential physician and medical philosopher of the Roman Empire.</note>, in the first book of his work *On the Elements*, explains this opinion in almost these exact words: 

*All philosophers admit that there is a certain **matter** underlying all the elements, which itself lacks any quality <note>original: "quae omni caret qualitate"</note>, and that the extreme heat of fire is the principle within it. Furthermore, they agree that this same matter is never created and never destroyed, but rather it is the qualities within the matter that perish and are born.* 

Later, in the same book, he continues: 

*For when extreme heat is added to matter, then that whole thing becomes the element [of fire]. In the same way, whenever extreme cold, moisture, or dryness is joined to matter—* <note>The text cuts off here as the author prepares to describe the formation of the other three elements.</note>

->C O M M E N D A T.<-

...and they are the simplest things, created, and perpetual, existing by themselves. All things are made from their mixture, and all things are ultimately resolved back into them. The imagination of those who claim that in all **transmutations** <term>Latin: *transmutatio* — the change of one substance into another, such as lead into gold or a seed into a plant.</term> of bodies, dissolution occurs into **Aristotle’s prime matter** <term>Latin: *materia prima* — in classical philosophy, the most basic, formless substrate of all reality.</term>, which is an incorporeal substance, is vain and false. Furthermore, they pretend that the limit of dissolution—the point where new composition begins—happens in a mere moment of time, which is in no way consistent with reason.

For instance, if a grain is dissolved by water on a floor or a board, it perishes and first produces a white or yellow sprout, then a green one. This sprout is born from the physical body of the grain, not from some incorporeal substance, as anyone can see for themselves. Likewise, from a **mustard seed** <note>original: *grano sinapeos* — a common reference to the inherent power of life contained within a tiny physical body.</note> a shrub is born; from **barley** <note>original: *hordeum*</note>, barley; and from an egg, a chick is generated, not a dog. This is an evident sign...

...things better known, either to us or to nature. To us, the things better known are effects, goals, individual things, and things in their whole and unrefined state <note>original: *confusæ*—referring to things perceived as a whole before their constituent parts are analyzed</note>, by which we argue through formal reasonings and **enthymemes** <term>A logical argument in which one of the premises is implied rather than explicitly stated.</term>. Whenever instruction arises from these things that are more familiar to us, it is called the **method of dissolution** <note>original: *methodus dissolutionis*</note>, known in Greek as **analysis** <note>original: *ἀνάλυσις*</note> or the **analytic method** <note>original: *μέθοδος ἀναλυτική*</note>. 

The other method, which is entirely the opposite, proceeds through causes, general principles, and constituent parts. Indeed, we argue by means of **inductions** <term>The process of reasoning from specific instances to a general law or conclusion.</term> and examples; in this case, the progression moves from the senses to the intellect, whereas in the former method, it is the reverse. Among the Latins, it is called **construction** or **composition**; the Greeks call it **synthesis** <note>original: *σύνθεσις*</note> or the **synthetic method** <note>original: *μέθοδος συνθετική*</note>. 

Nature teaches us these two methods at all times by composing and dissolving its own bodies; and human reason observes and employs them without difficulty in various matters, even if few people actually notice it. A third method, which shares in both of these, could not inconveniently...

This is achieved either by **division** <term>The logical process of breaking down a broad genus or category into its specific sub-types or species.</term> or by **definition** <term>The formal statement of the essential nature or meaning of a thing.</term>; for this reason, the method itself is called the **method of division and definition** <note>original: *methodus diuisionis & definitionis*</note>. 

Nevertheless, in truth, if you consider the whole business, it is entirely one and the same thing: because a whole is always presented and then explained by its parts, just as a definition is identical to the thing defined <note>original: *res definita*</note>, and all parts joined together are identical to the thing defined. 

The first two methods <note>Referring to synthesis (construction) and analysis (dissolution) described on the previous page.</note> belong to both nature and reason, but this third one belongs to reason alone, which reason employs in its own affairs whenever it cannot conveniently use either of the first two. 

Method is sought out from the intended goal: for if that goal is some whole entity, it must be taught through its causes by means of **construction** <note>original: *constructione*—the process of building up from causes to effects</note>, or through its parts by definition or division. If, however, the goal is a specific part, it must be taught by **dissolution** <note>original: *dissolutione*—the process of breaking an effect down into its causes</note>. ### COMMENDATION

### COMMENDATION

In the fourth book, the author returns once again to more general principles. He first treats a certain invisible virtue that provides immense benefit to the body; he calls this **balsam** <note>original: *balsamum*; in this context, it refers to an internal life-sustaining essence rather than just an ointment</note>. This balsam exceeds the entire physical nature of the body; it preserves two bodies in their union and sustains the celestial body together with the **four elements** <term>four elements: the traditional building blocks of the material world—earth, water, air, and fire</term>. 

This virtue exists in two ways: it either acts by chance and in secret, or it is obtained through the arts and through specific means, such as herbs, metals, **quintessences** <term>quinta essentia: the "fifth essence," a highly purified extract that contains the medicinal soul of a substance</term>, and other things. For a long life, he prescribes certain limits—not because every life can be extended indefinitely, but so that a person may consider the very limit that *can* be advanced. 

Indeed, there is a twofold limit: one is the fixed limit of natural life, which man, as a **Microcosm** <term>Microcosmus: "little world"; the Paracelsian belief that the human being is a miniature reflection of the entire universe</term>, cannot surpass. The other limit is one that can be crossed through specific medical means. He distinguishes a threefold **Iliastrum** <term>Iliastrum: a Paracelsian term for the primordial force or matter that governs longevity and the duration of life</term>, which must be diligently 

->considered<-

by the physician.

# VALENTINUS DE RE-
## TIIS SENDS GREETINGS TO THE PIOUS READER <note>original: pio Lectori S. (Salutem)</note>

**Theophrastus Paracelsus** <note>The famous Swiss physician, alchemist, and philosopher (1493–1541) known for pioneering the use of chemicals and minerals in medicine</note>, descended from a noble Swedish lineage and born among the **Hermits of Switzerland** <note>original: Eremitas Heluetiae; referring to Einsiedeln, Switzerland, home of a famous Benedictine abbey</note>, was called "Paracelsus the Great" by the Stoics. He wrote 230 books on **philosophy**, published 46 on the **solemnity of medicine**, revised 12 on the **State** <note>original: de Republica</note>, and composed 7 in the **mathematical art**. 

Furthermore, he combined three works together into a single volume titled the ***Theophrastia***, and authored 66 books on **hidden and abstruse** matters. The first work is titled ***Archidoxis*** <note>original: Archidoxis; meaning 'The Arch-Doctrines' or 'Supreme Teachings'</note>, in which he explains the extractions and separations of **virtues** <term>virtutum: in alchemical medicine, these are the active, healing essences or spiritual powers contained within physical matter</term> from the weak or base material. The second is titled ***Parafarchum***, in which he treats of the **highest good** <note>original: summo bono</note> in eternity. The third is titled ***Carboantes***, in which he explains **transmutations** in form.

<summary>Valentinus de Retiis provides a brief biography of Paracelsus and a massive bibliography of his works, highlighting his three most significant mystical and medical texts: the Archidoxis, Parafarchum, and Carboantes.</summary>

<keywords>Theophrastus Paracelsus, Valentinus de Retiis, Einsiedeln, bibliography, Archidoxis, Parafarchum, Carboantes, alchemy, medical virtues, transmutations</keywords>

<header>COMMENDATION</header>

...and to be. **Gellius Zemeus** <note>A contemporary scholar and admirer of Paracelsus</note> wrote concerning this German philosopher **Theophrastus** to **Passephallus Ceueus**: 

"Among the Germans there now exists a young man, whose equal the world does not produce, who has written so excellently in **philosophy**, **medicine**, and the **mathematical art**, as well as on the **State** <note>original: Republica; the governance and well-being of the commonwealth</note> and **justice**, that I believe there is either a wondrous influence in his birth-stars, or a greater grace of the **Holy Spirit** within him, or a vast presence of **spirits** <note>original: dæmonum; in the Renaissance context, this often refers to 'daemons' or guiding spirits/intelligences that provide hidden knowledge, not necessarily evil demons</note>. 

For it is beyond human capacity to investigate those things which he himself treats with such ease; I do not remember ever having read anyone more learned. Therefore, you, **Reader**, with a sincere mind,"

# THE FIRST BOOK OF THEOPHRASTUS
# OF HOHENHEIM, DOCTOR OF BOTH
## MEDICINES <note>original: utriusque medicinae; likely referring to the dual practice of internal medicine and surgery, or perhaps physical and spiritual healing</note>, CONCERNING
## LONG LIFE.

### CHAPTER I

<image-desc>Large historiated woodcut initial 'S' depicting a scholar or figure seated at a desk or bench within an architectural frame, with a landscape and building visible in the background.</image-desc>IF IT IS PERMISSIBLE for **Theophrastus** to philosophize concerning long life, I consider it first necessary, and worthy of being known, to define what life is—and especially indeed what immortal life is. This is a topic which the **Ancients** left entirely untouched; I believe it was either unknown to them or not sufficiently understood. This is why they have wished to provide for only the other kind of life—namely, the **mortal** life—until now. 

But so that I may finally define what life is: In truth, life is nothing else but a certain **Balsamic Mummy** <term>Mummia: in Paracelsian medicine, this refers not to an Egyptian corpse, but to a vital, internal "balsam" or life-force that preserves the body from decay</term>, preserving the mortal body from mortal worms and **corruption** <note>original: æstphara; a Paracelsian term for a specific kind of putrefaction or gangrenous decay</note>, with an impressed mixture of the **liquor of salts**. Furthermore, our life—that is, the long life—is that which neither spirit, nor li- <note>The text breaks mid-word; likely "lumen" (light) or "lux"</note>

<vocab>Theophrastus of Hohenheim, Paracelsus, Long Life, Balsamic Mummy, mixture of salts, doctor of medicine</vocab>

<summary>Paracelsus begins his treatise by defining life as an internal "balsam" or preservative force (Mummia) that protects the physical body from decay and worms. He argues that ancient philosophers only understood mortal life, whereas he intends to explore the nature of long and immortal life.</summary>
<keywords>Paracelsus, Long Life, Mummia, Balsam, preservation, medicine, philosophy, corruption, salts</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Balsamic Mummy" from the previous page. <page-num>2</page-num>

->**THEOPHRASTUS ON LIFE**<-

...light of nature <note>original: lumen naturae; the innate intelligence of the natural world</note> say is short. But for the ignorant it is indeed short, while through **art** <note>In this context, "Art" refers to the practice of alchemy and medicine</note> it is long. What is swifter than art? Yet what is longer than life—at least among those mortals who are not superstitious? Furthermore, what is more lasting, healthier, or more full of life than the **Balsam**? What is swifter, weaker, or more mortal than the physical body? Indeed, **temperament** is a "short" derived from a "long," and again a "long" from a "short." Why then is life long, and why is it short? 

Nevertheless, that life which is super-celestial physics follows no rules among us; the display of our own authority indicates only the mortal body, which is regulated (so to speak) by art unto the third term—indeed, even the fourth and fifth. <note>These "terms" likely refer to the stages of life or the "epochs" of health that a physician can extend through alchemical means, far beyond the natural expectations of the time.</note> 

This much regarding the living. What then of death? For what is death? It is certainly nothing other than the end of the **Balsam's** dominion, the destruction of the **Mummy** <term>Mummia: in Paracelsian medicine, the internal vital force or "spiritual lead" that preserves the body from corruption</term>, and the final matter of the **salts**. Once these immortal elements are separated from the mortal ones, it brings about the transitory return of the mortal limbs <note>a reference to the body returning to the dust from which it came</note>. 

This, therefore, is the "long life" spoken of from the beginning. This is also the "short life," namely that which is spoken of in relation to death. To us, death is death, not life. Yet **Art** is longer than this death. These things are the dissolutions of life, and also the digested separa-

<meta>catchword: rations (proportions/reasons)</meta>

<header>ON LONG LIFE, BOOK I.</header>

<sig>d 5</sig>

...the reasons for a pure, long, and healthy life, both mortal and immortal, which the day of birth joins and binds together from both bodies. For every union of mortal things—especially those of different natures—carries dissolution within it. Just as dissolution follows every union of natural things and those things which exist outside of nature, so too is dissolution the companion of the life that has been joined. 

For the cause of death is an **empirical war** <note>original: *bellum empiricum*; in Paracelsian philosophy, this refers to the constant, observable struggle between the vital forces and the agents of decay</note>, which is essentially a duel waged between the mortal and the immortal. This is clearly what is meant by the saying: *Each fights for his own homeland.* <note>original: *Pugnat quisq; pro patria*; an allusion to the internal elements struggling to return to their respective origins—the earthly to the earth and the divine to the heavens.</note> **Disease** is like a javelin, and the **Anthos** <term>Anthos: Greek for "flower"; in alchemical medicine, it refers to the "quintessence" or the most refined, protective essence of a substance</term> is the breastplate of war; aside from the strife within these, what else is there? This is the source and origin of this evil: the generation of disease, which is immediately followed by death. From these things, one may easily gather what life is, both mortal and immortal,

->and by what means both should be preserved,<-
->which will be explained more fully below<-
->at the end of the section on the<-
->physical body.<-

<summary>This page describes life as a fragile union of mortal and immortal elements that are constantly at "war" with one another, defining disease as the conflict and death as the eventual dissolution of this union.</summary>

<meta>This page transitions from the general theory of mortal and immortal life to the specific study of the physical human body. It establishes the "Physical Body" as the foundation for medical practice, emphasizing that while physical medicine cannot grant immortality, it is essential for maintaining health within our mortal frame.</meta>

<page-num>4</page-num>

<header>
THEOPHRASTUS <note>original: *Theoph.*, referring to Theophrastus Paracelsus</note> ON LIFE
CHAPTER II.
</header>

In order that all the things mentioned above might become more clearly known, I believe we must first speak of the **Physical body**. Indeed, the preservation of the physical body is the reason for everything discussed in our arguments. Moreover, the general practice <note>original: *praxis*; the application of medical knowledge</note> concerning the physical body ought to be conducted in this manner.

First, its parts are considered. These are studied through **local anatomy** <note>The study of specific regions of the body rather than general systems</note> throughout the entire physical body, along with an understanding of:
*   the marrows <note>original: *medullarum*; referring to both bone marrow and the "essence" within tissues</note>,
*   the conditions and the use of the **ligaments**,
*   the shapes of the **bones** and **cartilage**,
*   the **nerves**,
*   the properties of the **flesh**,
*   and the virtues <note>original: *virtutum*; the inherent powers or functional strengths</note> of the **seven principal members**. <note>In traditional medicine, these are the vital organs: the brain, heart, liver, lungs, kidneys, stomach, and spleen.</note>

All of this is necessary so that you may understand yourself according to this rule in what you are about to do within the physical realm. First, it is necessary that we have a thoroughly known understanding of the entire system and nature, both of the physical body and of physical life. For the body and the life of the physical man are **mortal**. From the mortal, truly, nothing can be drawn out that might produce a **long life** <note>original: *vitam longam*; here referring to the alchemical or spiritual extension of life beyond natural limits</note>; rather, the things that can be obtained from these mortal elements simply render the body healthy. And thus, neither the body, nor the mortal life,

<summary>Paracelsus introduces the study of anatomy as the essential foundation for medicine. He argues that while physical treatments can restore health, they are fundamentally limited because the physical body is mortal and cannot, on its own, produce true longevity.</summary>

<page-num>5</page-num>

<header>ON LONG LIFE, BOOK I.</header>

...in this our monarchy <note>In Paracelsian terms, "monarchy" refers to the sovereign system of the human body and its governing principles.</note> these things must be considered according to the principle of the **Arcanum** <term>Arcanum: a hidden, incorporeal essence or "secret" within a substance that has the power to heal or transform.</term> or the **Elixir**. <term>Elixir: a refined medicinal substance intended to prolong life.</term> For long life exists outside the body and is preserved by the body, and the lower body is preserved by it. Furthermore, when the body intervenes, the dissolution of both types of life <note>referring to the mortal and immortal aspects of life discussed previously</note> arises. For wherever mortality is not joined to immortality, there dissolution <note>The separation of life from the body</note> cannot occur. In this matter, the empirical muse, the sophisticated physicians, and the **spagyrists** <term>Spagyrists: alchemical physicians who used the processes of separation and recombination to create medicines.</term> have labored greatly—specifically on the question of how they might preserve the body like a **balsam** <term>Balsam: an internal, life-preserving fluid or force within the body.</term> so as not to cause death, since balsam is the **Mummy** <term>Mummia: a vital, curative power or "grease" within living things that prevents decay; not to be confused with Egyptian mummies.</term> of life, not of the body. Meanwhile, they are not mindful that death does not exist within life. For the death of life is nothing else but a certain dissolution from the immortal; when this occurs, then finally the body dies. And yet **Hippocrates** <note>The famous Greek "Father of Medicine," whom Paracelsus often critiqued for focusing too much on the physical humors rather than the spiritual essence.</note> directed all his extracts to this point, and he decided to take that little body in hand as the subject of long life, and to preserve the mortal with the mortal: even though there was never any life in the body that flowed from its own source. For the body is a created thing, but it is not life, and it is nothing less than the daughter of death. Therefore, life descends from the **Archaea** <term>Archaea (or Archeus): the internal "master-chemist" or vital force that governs the functions of an organ or the whole body.</term>, which is immortal. Not...

<summary>Paracelsus argues that life is an immortal essence (the Archaea) separate from the physical body, criticizing traditional Hippocratic medicine for trying to preserve the mortal body using only mortal means. He introduces alchemical concepts like Mummia and the Arcanum as the true keys to preserving the vital force that prevents physical decay.</summary>

--- Page 92 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument from page 5, which established that death is a dissolution between the mortal and immortal parts of a human. Here, Paracelsus (Theophrastus) critiques traditional Hippocratic medicine for only understanding the mortal "created" side of biology, while he proposes a celestial "body of life" that must be continually restored like a fire fed by wood.</meta>

<page-num>6</page-num>
<header>THEOPHRASTUS ON LIFE</header>

One must completely refer, you might say, the Hippocratic muse <note>The "Hippocratic muse" refers to traditional Greek medicine based on the teachings of Hippocrates, which Paracelsus often critiqued as being too focused on the mortal, physical aspects of the body.</note> to mortality. So be it; yet you may find a much easier path to health, since that **Great Mystery** <term>Magnale: a Paracelsian term for a great divine power or "great thing" that descends from the heavens to aid in healing.</term> descends from the heavens. For God granted Hippocrates nothing beyond created things; indeed, He did not even fully give him those remarkable mysteries that belong to created things.

But to the point. To this [mortal] body, God joined something else—consider it celestial—which exists in the body of life. Concerning this, I, Theophrastus, say: **This is the work, this is the labor** <note>original: *Hoc opus hic labor est* — a famous quotation from Virgil's *Aeneid*, referring to the difficulty of returning from the underworld; here, Paracelsus uses it to describe the difficulty of preventing the body's decay.</note> lest it break out into that dissolution <note>The separation of the soul/life from the body; death.</note> which belongs to mortals and is joined to this physical body alone.

And even if dissolution can happen in this fleeting body, and thus the loss of the celestial body results from it, it still cannot hinder long life because of the restoration that must continually occur, so that the body might completely lack any defect. For just as fire lives as long as wood is present, so long life exists as long as the body from the **ark** <note>original: *ex archa*. This likely refers to the *Archeus*, the "internal chemist" or life-force that Paracelsus believed governed the body's health and resided in the "ark" of the person.</note> is present, because the body must be preserved as a body through the intervention of the body that grows strong from without. For from that source it can be preserved. For the body is nothing other than the subject in which the long life of the eternal body grows.

<summary>Paracelsus argues that while traditional Hippocratic medicine only treats the mortal body, true health requires maintaining a "celestial body" within the physical one. He compares the preservation of life to a fire that must be constantly fed with "restoration" to prevent the dissolution of death.</summary>

<page-num>7</page-num>
<header>OF LONG [LIFE] BOOK I.</header>

### CHAPTER III.

So far we have dealt with the physical body; now we must speak of the reasoning by which its matter ought to be preserved from all corruption. In the first place, whatever the body destroys within itself must be restored from an external body, so that the supreme rule of the Spagyrists <term>Spagyrists: practitioners of the alchemical art of "separating and recombining," focusing on extracting pure medicinal essences from raw materials.</term> does not permit that common nature of balsam <term>Balsam: an internal life-sustaining fluid or preservative power Paracelsus believed existed in all living things to prevent decay.</term> through which it labors to preserve the body. 

For just as it is impossible for wood not to be consumed by fire, so it is impossible for the body not to be eventually corrupted by life itself <note>Paracelsus views the process of living as a consuming force, similar to how fire consumes fuel; the body is the "wood" that life eventually burns through.</note>. Therefore, we should give less credit to those experts in essential matters who claim that a long life is procured through balsam, when rather the nature of balsam is this: to preserve the body from its own corruption, so that no vacuum remains in the body. 

For every vacuum in those places is a disease or sickness in the body, and a kind of wasting away <note>original: *atrophia*</note> of a long life. For long life finds its place in a perfect body; in an imperfect one, it is dissolved into a continuous failure right up to death. For we know that the physical body can be sustained from death, and that very thing by the power of its own "mummy" <term>Mummy (Mummia): in Paracelsian medicine, the innate vital force or curative power within a body that can be used to heal wounds or resist decay.</term>...

<vocab>physical body, Spagyrists, balsam, mummy, long life, supreme rule of Spagyrists</vocab>

<summary>Paracelsus explains that the body is naturally consumed by the process of living, much like fire consumes wood. He argues that alchemical longevity depends on maintaining the body's internal "balsam" and "mummy" to prevent the "vacuums" or deficiencies that cause disease.</summary>

...born. These matters pertain to a healthy life, not a long one; for the limit is set for the physical body. It is worthwhile, however, that before we explain the long life, we should demonstrate and provide for the healthy life. These are the measures that defend against diseases. 

For instance, in "Persian Fire" <term>Persian Fire: a historical term (*ignis Persicus*) for inflammatory skin conditions like shingles, erysipelas, or anthrax.</term>, a limb perishes not without the loss of life, since that disease is the death of the limb and subsequently a cause for the [death of] life in the heart; and though this occurs by accident <note>original: *ab accidente*—referring to an external or chance occurrence rather than the natural end of one's lifespan</note>, it can nevertheless be removed through the physical balsam <term>Physical balsam: the body’s innate, semi-alchemical preservative fluid that Paracelsus believed resists decay and heals wounds.</term>. In cases of "mola" <term>Mola: a "false pregnancy" or a fleshy growth within the uterus.</term> and "hermia" <note>likely *hernia* (a rupture or protrusion of an organ)</note>, the long life is stripped away for as long as that same humor <term>Humor: one of the bodily fluids thought in medieval medicine to determine health and temperament.</term> persists. Similarly, in the carbuncle <term>Carbuncle: a severe, painful cluster of boils.</term> and the "sapphire" <note>original: *sapphiro*—likely referring to a bluish pestilential sore or a specific type of carbuncle associated with the plague</note>, it spreads more than is proper. 

It must be observed, therefore, that corruption is to be removed from the body, and what has flared up in the long life must be brought back again to a state of cooling. For this reason, in this place, the "specifics of nature"—which are prepared for this very purpose—must fortify the body whenever it is worn down by disease from any source; this is what is said to be the duty of physicians. 

In the long life, however, nothing of that sort is required. For even "Leonina" <term>Leonina: a form of leprosy (*facies leonina*) that gives the face a lion-like appearance.</term> can be restored to a long life on equal terms with one who is free from diseases. The same applies to "Lithyasis" <term>Lithyasis: the formation of stony concretions in the body, such as kidney or bladder stones.</term> and "Tympanites" <term>Tympanites: a condition where the abdomen is stretched tight like a drum due to gas or fluid.</term>: but these things concern the long life. In such a state, death is not at hand. For insofar as the physical body is committed to the physician’s care, death is present [in the members], but it is not present in the celestial body. ### CHAPTER IV.

Since, by the nature of creation, the body and its physical life pass into the composition of form as a single part, and because the physical body is but a half, while the celestial is the whole: the physician must therefore consider more deeply from where and from what sources the **greater man** <term>Greater Man: In Paracelsian philosophy, the "Greater Man" (Macrocosm) refers to the entire universe, which serves as the blueprint and source of life for the "Lesser Man" (Microcosm), the individual human being.</term> is to be preserved. For indeed, long life consists in the **greater life**. But in the lesser life dwells the subject of mortality, and this is introduced according to predestination—both the celestial life and body being joined to the physical body, which follows this union like an inseparable companion. From which indeed—

<page-num>11</page-num>
<header>ON LONG LIFE, BOOK I.</header>

Regarding predestination, it must be understood that although things are innumerable—one being union, another necessity <note>original: *oportet* — literally "it is necessary," referring to the internal logic or fate of a thing</note>, another the nature of things, another life, and another death—nevertheless, all of these are assigned to a predestined limit or an appointed point. For the Creator <term>original: *conditor* — The Founder or Architect of the universe</term> has decreed to hold this under His own privileged hand, so that He keeps the decree within Himself; thus, He may freely dispose of what He wills and what seems good, without His judgment being revoked.

Furthermore, when the union of these two forms is brought about—namely, the natural form and that which is beyond nature—into the "form of nature," and this is entirely drawn out within the **chamber of the womb** <term>original: *cella matricis* — the uterus, viewed here as a sacred vessel where the physical and spiritual are forged together</term>, two fathers procreate one son, and two mothers procreate one daughter; thus, these four persons <note>In Paracelsian thought, a child is the product of two sets of "parents": the physical human parents and the "Great Parents" (the Sun/Heaven as father and the Earth/Nature as mother).</note> perform the act of generation. This celestial seed, together with the mortal seed, gives birth to that living creature which appears born: it is elemental seed and super-celestial seed at once. 

For in this place, the bodily seed truly operates, which is what must be preserved in the maintenance of the natural path. However, that form which is "beyond nature" must be considered first and foremost, so that even those things which are beyond nature may be preserved through it. In this way, the child is registered as the heir of twofold goods: of nature, and of the essence which is from nature...

But so that the **physician** <term>original: *medicus*</term> may have something he can truly use, and not follow the trifles of the unskilled—who have written most lifelessly <note>original: *frigidissime* — literally "most coldly," implying a lack of spiritual insight or "vital heat" in their theories</note> on this matter—the physician must first be advised to apply his mind more to those things which are **beyond nature** than to those things which occur **according to nature**. 

Next, let him be thoroughly skilled in **predestination**. For from this, as if from a spring, flows that **sovereignty** <term>original: *monarchia* — in Paracelsian philosophy, a "monarchy" is a governing power or vital force that rules over a specific domain of the body or nature</term> which is beyond nature, rather than that which is merely specific and qualified <note>referring to the tangible, physical traits of a person</note>. ...they may grant. To make these matters more clearly understood, take this analogy. We see that in a person overcome by sleep, only the **life of nature** <note>original: *vitam naturæ* — the basic biological functions shared with all living things</note> is present, and the fate of the **spirits** <term>original: *spirituum* — in this context, the subtle vital forces that mediate between the soul and the physical body</term> plays along with it. Therefore, wherever the present life exists [in sleep], it does not exist fully, and indeed it exists without power. Although that life may be "dead" because it does not act or perform work, it is nevertheless implanted in the body. Likewise, in a person who offers his throat to the sword, the blow takes away **long life** <note>original: *vitam longam* — for Paracelsus, this is not just "living a long time," but a specific spiritual-vital essence that can be preserved or lost</note>. Furthermore, whatever still leaps with life in the body [immediately after a fatal blow] is the inborn life of nature. This natural life is not what we should observe, but rather that life by which the body is restored. Moreover, "long life" exists within us like a man, or as fire is implanted in wood, by which a man is **renewed** <note>original: *redintegrafcit* — literally "made whole again" or restored to a pristine state</note>.

From these things it is easily evident that man is born from a **double seed** <note>the physical seed of the biological parents and the spiritual seed from the divine/celestial source</note>. ...temperament <term>original: *complexio* — in medieval medicine, the "complexion" or "temperament" was the specific balance of the four humors in an individual</term> together with the nature of generations in the flesh, on account of the unsuitable and untimely operations of persons in their contrary nature. For it is evident that neither **melancholy** was inborn in us from the beginning <note>original: *principiò* — referring to the original, perfect state of humanity before the Fall or before physical corruption</note>, nor were the **sanguine**, **choleric**, or **phlegmatic** states. If that quality had remained alone and in its own degree, then bile would still prevail among us.

Therefore, the physician should hold no belief at all regarding the **four temperaments**. They did not exist in **Adam** <note>In Paracelsian philosophy, Adam represents the "Limbus" or the primordial, perfect material from which man was made, which was free from the imbalances of the four humors</note>, and much less in his offspring; nor can those four diverse things truly coexist with one another. Furthermore, just as individual temperaments are corrupted when an untimely birth intervenes—and this does not happen without the loss of children (for what is a "temperament"? It is the nature of the parent, and that nature exists without being "hot" or "cold," "black" or "white" <note>Paracelsus is refuting the Galenic idea that health is a balance of opposing qualities like heat, cold, moisture, and dryness</note>)—so also in that body which is beyond nature, a certain **hereditary seed**...

He asserts that a true physician should look past these "temperaments" to the unified nature of the parent and the "hereditary seed."</summary>

<page-num>15</page-num>

...is hereditary <note>original: *hereditarium* — continuing from the catchword on the previous page</note>. And if two people of the same **temperament** <term>original: *complexio* — the specific balance of humors in a person</term> are joined, a **supernatural seed** is nevertheless truly joined now, within which both wisdom and life are hidden. Therefore, marriage is twofold: one which human reason has persuaded, and the other which God has joined. The former is not properly a marriage, except insofar as the eyes and the grasp of nature allow. In that human union, although a person looks in every direction for a justification for himself and his children, and may claim to seek the Spirit of God as the one who joined them, or may pretend toward honor, it is nevertheless nothing but **hypocrisy**. The proof of this is the constant and sudden change of circumstances <note>original: *mutatio locorum* — literally "change of places," likely referring to the instability and transience of human-driven relationships compared to divine ones</note>.

However, this union which **God has joined** is what truly exists as marriage. It is related to the **long life** <note>In this context, "long life" refers to Paracelsus's concept of a vital, spiritual health that transcends the common decay of the body</note> because of the "divorce" <note>original: *divortium* — here meaning a separation or breaking apart of the vital forces</note> which cannot happen in this state; this is something which no one understands except through the intervention of children. For this reason, many are even sanctified in their mother's womb.

These are the ones whom God has joined, such as the wife of **Uriah** and **David**. This was so even though this union diametrically conflicted with what the human mind persuaded itself was a "just and legitimate marriage" <note>Paracelsus refers to the biblical story of King David and Bathsheba. Though their union began in adultery and the death of her husband Uriah, Paracelsus suggests a hidden divine purpose or "supernatural seed" was at work, as they became ancestors of the Messiah</note>. Because, however, both of them—beyond that which belonged to nature—as if by heredita-

<summary>Paracelsus distinguishes between marriages made by human reason, which he labels as hypocritical and unstable, and those joined by God, which involve a "supernatural seed." ...attained a long life <note>original: *vitam longam*</note> through marriage, for the sake of Solomon, who could not have been born from any other source except Bathsheba; God joined David’s seed to her, even though she was an adulteress <note>original: *meretrice* — Paracelsus uses this term to emphasize that God’s providential choice transcends human morality or "natural" expectations</note>. These things are set down here so that those matters which are beyond nature may be fully revealed to the physician.

### CHAPTER VI.

Therefore, since the practice is divided into two—one part concerning physics <note>meaning standard medicine or the study of the physical body</note> and the other concerning long life—the physician will determine how the use of each is applied based on the intended goal. However, concerning this life which is beyond nature, which we are discussing at present, we must see whether it is possible for us to attain it by some means within our physical life. This is necessary because it exists outside of the powers that follow nature, and the **secrets of long life** <term>original: *longæ vitæ arcana* — the "arcana" are the hidden, inner virtues or spiritual essences of things that Paracelsus sought to extract</term> lie hidden within it. For in this state, the impressions that are beyond nature become evident, so that they coincide within a supernatural life, just as the **firmament** <term>firmament: In this context, the celestial or "astral" forces of the stars that Paracelsus believed were mirrored within the human body</term> [concurs] into the body...

...which is according to nature, concurs.
