For this reason, these phenomena have begun to be called "impressions" by some, "incantations" by others, "superstitions" by yet others, and by various other names according to the rules of the **magical art** <term>magical art: *magica ars* — for Paracelsus, this is not sorcery, but the highest branch of medicine that deals with the spiritual forces of nature</term>. For from these things flows that which the Greeks call **magyria** <term>magyria: a Greek-derived term Paracelsus uses to describe the practical application of spiritual knowledge</term>; it deals with nothing other than those impressions which they call incantations and superstitions, and it directs itself toward the **supernatural body**.

Therefore, it is worthwhile to speak of the supernatural body within its own impressions. For we see that the whole of magic is twisted by **Astronomers** <note>here referring to those who study the stars only in a physical or mathematical sense, rather than the spiritual "inner stars"</note> for an improper use, and is wrongly labeled by everyone as superstition or as a kind of **pharmaceutria** <term>pharmaceutria: literally "poisoning" or "sorcery with drugs"; Paracelsus complains that true magic is being confused with common witchcraft</term>.

In the same way, they have entirely classified **necromancy** <term>necromancy: the practice of communicating with the spirits of the dead</term> and **nigromancy** <term>nigromancy: "black magic" or "black divination"; often used interchangeably with necromancy in this period</term> here, so that both are believed to be a form of idolatry. Yet these things remain entirely silent unless a certain **influence** <note>original: *influentia* — the spiritual or celestial power that flows into the material world</note> intervenes. For although the **shades of the dead** <term>original: *manes* — the spirits or ghosts of the departed</term> may respond or speak from every direction, this does not happen unless an influence that is beyond nature intervenes. This is something which is wrongly believed to be an **imposture of Satan** <note>Paracelsus argues that these phenomena are part of a hidden, divinely-ordered nature, rather than being mere demonic tricks</note>, because such a thing would be impossible for a human...

### CHAPTER VIII

But so that the physician <note>original: *physicus*; here meaning a natural philosopher or student of nature</note> may have all things fully and perfectly, let us look at the examples of the elders, who labored greatly in this matter of magic to attain a long life. They did this without any admixture of Hermetic rejuvenation <note>original: *repuerascentiæ*; literally "becoming a boy again," referring to alchemical processes intended to restore youth</note>, and without the art <gloss>a</gloss> of spagyric experience <term>spagyric: the alchemical practice of separating and recombining natural substances to create potent medicines, particularly associated with the school of Paracelsus</term>, which belongs to the body alone. 

Let us therefore look at the age of Adam and Methuselah, who are a significant part of the principles of the magical art. Although the common people are persuaded of this—that the first-formed man <note>original: *protoplastum*; a term for Adam as the first molded or created human</note> had the longest lifespan, and [future] man shall have the shortest. 

This last point, which is an effective argument in their schools, should by no means be approved in the way it is held concerning the age of men. The cause of Adam's great age was magic, from whose influence he lived continually. We consider, therefore, the wretched death of Adam, forever to be deplored by his descendants, not so much because of the Fall, as [much as...] <note>The sentence continues onto the next page.</note>

### CHAPTER IX.

There is moreover another method for preserving a long life, which Muhammad <note>original: *Mahumet*</note> wrote down for his people according to magic and granted for many years; and this did not come from God, but from that influence which is beyond nature. Because Muhammad, as a magus <note>a practitioner of magic or hidden wisdom</note>, practiced this for the unlearned common people and not just for himself for some time, he earned the reputation of immortality. This atheist <note>original: *atheus*; here used to denote a non-Christian or "heathen" practitioner rather than a modern lack of belief in God</note> preserved himself for some years over a hundred, which was regarded as a vice in him and categorized as idolatry. These magi <note>practitioners of hidden or divine wisdom</note> were followed by others who falsely advertised themselves under this nearly divine title. Among them was Hippocrates <note>original: *Hippocrates*; likely referring to a legendary figure in alchemical lore rather than the historical Greek physician</note>, who transformed his daughter into a body entirely foreign to nature, outside of any natural influence, because he preferred that she remain forever in her current form; this serves as clear evidence of an incantation <note>original: *incantatio*; a magical spell or ritual chant</note>.

In the same way, Ierellus <note>possibly a reference to a legendary figure or alchemist found in occult literature</note> retained a long life and joined his mind to a metamorphosis <term>metamorphosis: a supernatural transformation of the physical form</term> of nature. The origin and knowledge of these men will be described in their proper places.

Furthermore, in the preservation of the body that exists beyond nature, many were the equals of Methuselah <note>the biblical figure said to have lived 969 years</note>, but they erred greatly regarding transformations. For their work devolved into a "fantastic" <note>original: *phantasticum*; here meaning illusory or existing only in the imagination rather than true physical reality</note> body, due to an ignorance of physical realities. Therefore, there are many whose long lives endured until the very last day; however, such transformations can also occur without a long life. Among these was **Styrus**, who, when he was struggling for his life, is said to have drawn out the strength and nature of a certain robust young man who happened to be standing nearby. He achieved this through the power of **imagination** <note>to Paracelsus, imagination was not just "make-believe" but a potent force capable of physical effects</note> in such a way that his senses, thoughts, and finally his very soul were transferred into the other. Through this same imagination, the **Archaeus** <term>Archaeus: the "inner master-builder" or vital force that governs the biological and chemical functions of the body</term> is said to have drawn in the knowledge and wisdom of every learned and prudent man. Such is the power of the soul in which that supernatural vigor exists, that sometimes it satisfies a burning and even growing desire. From this comes the contempt...

# LONG LIFE, BOOK I.

...that power of images and **gamahaeas** <term>gamahaeas: also spelled *gamaheu* or *gamahé*; these are natural stones or talismans believed to be "imprinted" with celestial or magical powers, often bearing shapes or marks resembling stars or figures</term> among those who have abused this image even unto destruction. From this source come those words, characters, signs, forms, and gestures of the hands, curses and prayers, which are the primary causes of incantation—and indeed of those words which are commonly used to treat wounds and other diseases. Finally, whatever can be done in this manner occurs through the power of that **supernatural body** <note>original: *quod præter naturã est, corporis*; refers to the "astral" or "sidereal" body which Paracelsus believed existed alongside the physical body</note> implanted within us. 

Furthermore, from these supernatural impressions, even the stars of the firmament—Venus and Saturn, and the other planets—burst forth, so that this supernatural influence rules and governs the **lower stars** <note>original: *astra inferiora*; in Paracelsian philosophy, these are the internal "stars" or spiritual centers within the human body that correspond to the planets in the sky</note>. Therefore, whatever occurs in the gamahaea and in imaginations, when the planets and signs are approaching, all of this must be referred back to the higher signs. For this reason, those bodies which are frail and thus subject to death can easily be liberated from death by that supernatural power. Moreover, Venus and Saturn, Mars and Mercury, as they perform their courses in the upper firmament, have granted immortality to many mortals—and this indeed without any effort...

ON LONG LIFE, Book Two.
<sig>f 2</sig>

### CHAPTER I.

In the previous volume, we treated those matters concerning long life in general; here we shall treat them specifically—namely, a certain unique method of healing<gloss>a</gloss>, which is customarily achieved through the intervention of **specific secrets** <term>arcana: Paracelsus used this term to describe the concentrated, hidden healing virtues or "quintessences" found within natural substances</term>, and others of that kind, even outside the context of long life, such as in cases of **apoplexy** <note>original: *apoplexia*; a historical term for a sudden loss of consciousness or paralysis, usually associated today with a stroke</note>, wounds, and similar ailments. For you must prefer a complete and healthy life even to a long one, so that the foundation upon which all health and long life rests might become known. 

The status and summary of this second book, therefore, is that a whole life may be preserved and never break out into **corruption** <note>original: *corruptionem*; in this context, it refers to the metabolic breakdown, decay, or "rusting" of the body’s vitality</note>. This is why, in the first place, the power of the **secret** <note>original: *arcani*</note> is required, and then the nature of the **balsam** <term>balsam: in Paracelsian medicine, the "internal balsam" is a life-preserving fluid or spirit within the body that prevents decay and heals wounds</term>. For health is the companion of every secret. 

In this sense, you must understand the subject of long life. We have therefore divided the second book into two parts: for the body is either strong or weak. If it is strong, it must be preserved in that same health—

<vocab>Long Life, method of healing, specific secrets, apoplexy, health, balsams, body</vocab>

<summary>Paracelsus introduces Book Two by shifting from general theories to specific medical methods, arguing that maintaining a body free from "corruption" is the true foundation of a long life. He introduces the "arcana" (healing secrets) and the "balsam" (the body's natural preservative) as the primary focus for treating both strong and weak bodies.</summary>

# ON LONG LIFE
Second Book.

### CHAPTER I.

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'I' featuring foliate and floral motifs within a square frame.</image-desc>

In the previous volume, where we treated long life in general, here we shall treat it specifically—namely, a certain unique method of healing <note>original: "medendi"</note>, which is customarily done through the intervention of specific arcana <term>arcana: Paracelsus's term for the hidden, potent "secrets" or quintessences extracted from nature that possess extraordinary healing power</term> and other things of that kind, even beyond [the pursuit of] long life, such as in apoplexy <note>apoplexy: a historical term for a sudden loss of consciousness or a stroke</note>, wounds, and other such conditions. For it is necessary that you prefer a complete and indeed healthy life to a long one, so that the foundation upon which all health and long life rests may become known. Therefore, the status and summary of this second book is that a whole life may be preserved and never break out into corruption <note>corruption: the process of physical decay or the breakdown of the body's natural balance</note>. This is why, in the first place, the virtue of the arcanum is required, and then the nature of the balsam <term>balsam: in Paracelsian medicine, the "internal balsam" is a vital preservative force within the body that resists disease and aging</term>. For health is the companion of every arcanum. You ought to accept the subject of long life according to this view. Therefore, we have divided this second book into two parts: for the body is either firm or infirm. If it is firm, it must be preserved in that same health—

->THEOPHRASTUS ON LIFE<-

...althy state. But if it is infirm, or if some form of sickness lies hidden within it, you must reduce the individual genera together with their species to some notable medical canon, which they call a **Magisterium** <term>Magisterium: a Paracelsian term for a "mastery" or a sovereign remedy prepared chemically to exert a powerful, broad effect on the body</term>. This is a specific method of healing, although by a much more accurate nomenclature—and for another reason—it might be called *universal*, because of the species; for if these are gathered under one genus, they no longer constitute a specific cure, but a universal one. 

However, there is something more general which will be mentioned at the end of this book, which embraces all genera into one, just as species are embraced by particulars. Of this kind is the method of curing fever <note>original: "febris"</note>, which includes within itself synocha <note>synocha: a continuous, non-intermittent fever</note>, ephemeral <note>ephemeral: a fever typically lasting only a single day</note>, hectic, causon <note>causon: a severe, burning inflammatory fever</note>, and other species. Similarly for dropsy <note>original: "hydropisis," a historical term for swelling caused by excess fluid, now called edema</note>, which contains ascites, tympanites, and hyposarca <note>Paracelsus distinguishes between fluid in the abdomen (ascites), gas/bloating (tympanites), and fluid in the flesh or connective tissues (hyposarca)</note>. Likewise for gout <note>original: "gutta," literally "the drop," based on the old belief that disease "dropped" into the joints</note>, lethargy, convulsions <note>original: "tortura"</note>, paralysis, and apoplexy. 

Something even more universal, however, encloses within itself gout, fever, dropsy, and all other diseases. And that is the **arcanum** <term>arcanum: a hidden, celestial virtue or "secret" essence extracted from nature that acts as a powerful restorative</term>, which we dedicated in the *Archidoxis* <note>original: "Archidoxis," referring to Paracelsus's *Archidoxis Magicae* or *Archidoxa*, his foundational texts on the secrets of nature and alchemy</note>. And the tincture which drives out the leprous humor <note>humor: in medieval medicine, a fluid or influence; here, the "corruption" found in base metals</note> of metals works in no dissimilar way...

<summary>Paracelsus explains how various diseases can be grouped into broad categories (genera) and treated with "Magisteries" or "Arcana"—universal remedies that address the root cause of many symptoms at once.</summary>

### LONGA LIB. II.

...[is reduced], and it shines when reduced to its elemental state in every direction, just like fire, which consumes whatever it seizes. For there are no species nor any genera of things that are not seized by fire. The professors of the spagyric art <term>spagyric art: the alchemical practice of separating, purifying, and recombining natural substances to create more potent medicines</term> looked toward this principle, asserting that medicines <note>original: "medicamina"</note> should only then be applied to the body once they have been reduced to fire. <margin>Just as fire consumes all wood, so a universal medicine consumes all diseases.</margin> For through this element, every sickness must be consumed by any physician.

Although mention is made elsewhere of potable gold <term>potable gold: "aurum potabile," a legendary alchemical solution of gold believed to be a universal cure and an elixir of life</term> and similarly of the quintessence <term>quintessence: the "fifth essence," the purest and most concentrated part of a substance, separated from the four base elements</term>, nevertheless, here we refer these individual things to the *cheyri* <note>cheyri: a specialized Paracelsian term for a medicinal essence, often associated with the "mercury" of a substance or the "flower" of a plant</note> and to the sapphire flower <note>original: "sapphiricum anthos," a poetic alchemical term for a highly refined mineral extract or essence</note>. This is a point that has deceived many, among whom is Arnold <note>Arnold of Villanova (c. 1240–1311), a major medieval physician and alchemist; Paracelsus often cites him to either correct his theories or acknowledge his influence</note> along with his rivals, who produced only sublunary things <note>sublunary: belonging to the earthly realm below the moon, which in the old worldview was the domain of change, decay, and imperfection</note> in this regard.

<margin>The singular method of healing, and the means by which it is achieved.</margin> In this second book, we shall describe a singular method of healing in both forms of medicine, adding the reasons by which you may discover it, concerning the origin of diseases. At the end, the *cheyri* and the balsam are added, which require a reader who is highly skilled in these matters.

## On Gout. Chapter I.

There are three things that are required for the perfect cure of gout <term>gout: historically called "podagra," a painful condition where "excess humors" were thought to collect in the joints, particularly the feet</term>: Purging, opening, and then the final treatment, according to this opinion. First, let every gout sufferer be purged with a full and perfect purging, and that specifically with the coral secret remedy <gloss>a</gloss> <term>coral secret remedy: known as the "arcanum corallinum," this was a high-level alchemical preparation, often involving mercury or coral, intended to "drive out" the root of a disease</term>. By the intervention of this remedy, the gouty fluxes <note>original: "fluxus podagrae"; the "flows" of harmful fluids that physicians believed caused the swelling</note> are drawn out, to such an extent that no place is left henceforth for the gout to take hold. In this coral remedy, which is made from the essence of gold, there is such power and virtue that it is impossible for a physician to grasp its effect except through this method of purging. Let this method of purging be performed six or seven times, depending on the age, the hardness, or the nature of the gout.

Furthermore, if any remnants of the disease remain, or if the gout sufferer is otherwise too weak to bear such a purging, let an opening be made in this manner: where the center of the gout lies hidden, open it, and do so with the intervention <gloss>b</gloss> of the alkali of spirits <term>alkali of spirits: a caustic or sharp chemical substance used to create a "fontanelle" or artificial sore to allow the disease to drain</term>. Immediately upon this opening, a swelling arises, beneath which whatever was serving the center of the disease flows together, and it breaks out during the casting off <gloss>c</gloss> of the eschar <term>eschar: a piece of dead tissue or a dry crust—like a scab—produced by the caustic "alkali"</term>. Moreover, once the gouty flux has ceased, then at last apply the complete cure, and indeed the consolidation—

<vocab>Gout, Purging, Coral remedy, Essence of gold, Alkali of spirits, Eschar</vocab>

<summary>This page outlines a rigorous three-step protocol for treating gout using alchemical "arcana," specifically emphasizing repeated purging with a gold-based coral remedy and the use of caustic "alkali" to drain the physical site of the ailment.</summary>
<keywords>gout, podagra, purging, arcanum corallinum, essence of gold, alchemical medicine, eschar, medical drainage, Paracelsian therapy</keywords>

[...opening] proceed with the treatment, namely with poultices <term>poultice: a soft, moist mass, often heated and medicated, applied to the body to treat inflammation</term> made from mummia <term>mummia: in Paracelsian medicine, this is not an Egyptian mummy, but rather a "vital balsam" or essence—either harvested from a body or prepared alchemically—believed to have supreme restorative powers</term>. These are the things that heal the gout. Although $^d$ <gloss>original: "d"</gloss> the liquor of mummia, when properly prepared, may even heal the disease without an opening if the case is recent and has not yet hardened; nevertheless, this occurs more by its native power, as if by its specific nature. The third method of healing gout is by means of mummia—mummia reduced into its ultimate alkalized matter and the liquor of mummia—according to this principle: Anoint the gouty limbs daily, and do so for seven or eight weeks, depending on the condition and nature of the gout. This three-fold method of healing restores whatever is mutilated, or whatever is crippled, to its former health. This is a new method of driving out disease, I know for certain, especially among the dull and unlearned physicians, and it is contrary to their rules—as those mockers argue, who have gained nothing from the medical profession except an empty title.

### On Gout, Chapter II. <note>original: "De Gutta Cap. II." The term "gutta" literally means "drop" and was used for gout and other conditions believed to be caused by a "dropping" of harmful humors.</note>

To drive away the ailments that arise from gout and the various types of gout—such as the "lethargic disease" <note>lethargicus: a state of pathological drowsiness or coma</note>, paralysis of the tongue or limbs, etc., apoplexy <term>apoplexy: a sudden loss of consciousness or movement, usually a stroke</term>, facial twisting, and other such forms—a general cure is required. For in all of these, one ought to consider nothing other than the power of nature. Neither purging, nor a simple tonic <term>tonic: a strengthening medicine; original "confortatiua"</term>, nor anointing, nor anything else in which they have labored so much—both Valescus de Taranta <note>a famous 14th-century Portuguese physician and author of the "Philonium"</note> and those who came before him—is sufficient.

Therefore, we must first observe in what state of health that power of nature is found, by which we wish to remedy the gout. However, before we come to those matters, it must be understood that gout, as far as its quantity is concerned, contains no more than one scruple <term>scruple: a tiny unit of weight used by apothecaries, about 1.3 grams</term> within itself, and apoplexy does not quite reach one full scruple. There is no reason, then, for us to observe the long-winded "Canons" of Avicenna <note>the "Canon of Medicine" by the Persian physician Ibn Sina, which was the standard medical textbook in Europe for centuries</note>, since there is so very little of the substan-

<meta>The word "substan-" is completed by the catchword "tia" on the next page, forming "substantia" (substance).</meta>

<summary>This page introduces a broader category of diseases related to 'gout'—including strokes and paralysis—and argues that these conditions are caused by tiny amounts of physical matter, making the massive textbooks of traditional medicine unnecessary.</summary>

[ON LONG-LASTING DISEASES] BOOK II.

...substance the disease has within itself. <note>original: "...tia [substantia] in se habeat morbus." This completes the sentence from the previous page suggesting the physical quantity of the ailment is minute.</note> Nor is it possible for the locations and the pains to be grasped by those [traditional remedies]. For this reason, a strengthening medicine <term>strengthening medicine: original "confortatiua," a category of medicine intended to fortify the body's internal organs</term> must be prepared in such a way that it specifically expels nature and, by its own nature, overcomes it.

For as often as the "drop" <note>original: "gutta," referring to the gouty humor</note> chokes the vessels in the lungs, so often apoplexy immediately follows along with the choking—a condition which nothing but secret remedies <term>secret remedies: original "arcana," the higher-order chemical medicines Paracelsus championed over herbal mixtures</term> can remove. From this, I think it is clear enough that even a tiny bit of mystery in the medical art is worth more than all the "forms" of Avicenna <note>a famous 11th-century Persian physician whose "Canon of Medicine" dominated medieval thought</note>, more than the entire spice-shop <note>original: "myropolium," a derogatory reference to traditional apothecaries</note>, and more than both "Lights" <note>Refers to the *Lumen Apothecariorum* (Light of Apothecaries), a standard pharmaceutical manual</note> and the *Praepositus* <note>Nicolaus Praepositus, author of a major medieval book on antidotes</note>, together with that "notable experience" which the *Thesaurus* <note>The *Thesaurus Pauperum* (Treasure of the Poor), a widely used medical handbook</note> prescribes. By these things, almost the entire world has been deceived until now.

But to return to the secret remedies: the secret remedy is twofold. One is vitriolated <note>derived from vitriol or sulfuric acid compounds</note>, and the other is the "mercury of gold." The common crowd of Salernitan physicians <note>doctors from the famous medical school at Salerno, Italy</note> falsely calls this latter one "Potable Gold" <note>original: "Aurum potabile," a legendary alchemical elixir</note>. They are led by an opinion of Hermes <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the mythical founder of alchemy</note> which they use as a pretext for their own ignorance. Indeed, they are such "excellent" men that they take the "horizon" to be potable gold, judging Hermes solely by their own lack of skill. Other secret remedies are to be used externally, such as balsam, aloes, and chebulic myrobalans <note>a type of medicinal fruit from India used as a purgative</note>.

## LONGA BOOK II.

The same applies to the medicine for leprosy: for which reason we ought to undertake a full and perfect cure, for the sake of preparing regeneration, and this very thing is done according to the prescription of the spagyric tincture <term>spagyric tincture: a concentrated medicinal extract produced by the alchemical process of separating, purifying, and recombining a substance's essential components</term>, in this manner: In the beginning, the "metals of the lepers" <note>original: "metalla leprosorum"; a metaphor used in alchemy where the diseased human body is compared to "impure" or "sick" metals that must be purified</note> must be cleansed through the intervention of a tincture. Within this, a double tincture and a double leprosy lie hidden—namely red and white. The spagyrics call the latter "lunar" and the former "solar": the first is obtained from the tincture of the sun <note>original: "tinctura solis," referring to gold</note>, the second from the tincture of the moon <note>original: "tinctura lunae," referring to silver</note>.

Here I do not think it is worth the effort to observe those things which the ancients wrote regarding the origin of leprosy, since practice itself <note>original: "praxis," referring to clinical experience or laboratory work</note> brings the truth. Therefore, in this place, we must act in a spagyric manner, and we should not at all observe what was written by the ancients about the origin of leprosy.

Furthermore, he who wishes to consult the spagyric art regarding leprosy should take care that he does not wander astray with those who falsely believed the "mercury of the sun" <note>original: "mercurium solis," a purified alchemical essence of gold</note>—which most spagyrics call the "First Matter"—to be "potable gold" <term>potable gold: original "aurum potabile," a legendary alchemical elixir made of liquid gold, believed to be a universal cure-all</term>. These people were deceived by the outcome, just as Archelaus <note>likely Archelaus of Athens, an ancient philosopher, or a namesake cited in medieval alchemical texts</note> maintains. We, however, assert in this place that "red leprosy" is healed by the mercury of the sun, and "white leprosy" is healed by the mercury of the moon. This is the work, this is the labor <note>original: "Hoc opus, hic labor est," a famous quote from Virgil’s Aeneid (VI.129), used here to emphasize the extreme difficulty of the task</note>: to prepare the mercury exactly. Finally, and...

### On Epilepsy, Chapter 4.

As far as surgery is concerned, the discourse is twofold: the first part pertains to preparing a "falling" emunctory <term>emunctory: an anatomical opening or "drain" used to expel waste or diseased humors from the body</term>, and the second to drawing out the epileptic humor <note>in ancient medicine, a "humor" was a bodily fluid believed to govern health and temperament; an excess or corruption of it caused disease</note> according to this instruction: Open the skull with instruments suitable for the task. Next, after a silver pipe has been placed in its proper location, heal the wound and restore the patient to their former life. Now, every falling sickness <note>original: "morbus caducus"; a traditional term for epilepsy</note> equipped with an emunctory of this kind is unable to pass into a paroxysm <note>a sudden attack or seizure</note>. This concludes the first way. Now to the second. Dissolve the limb corrupted by epilepsy, or the upper part of the limb—for instance, the ligaments and the surrounding arteries—and do this with a corrosive narcotic <note>a substance that both numbs pain and dissolves tissue</note> or a corroding salt, without pain, all the way down to the bones. Then, heal it once more: immediately, narcotic ligaments, arteries, and finally the flesh itself are born anew, which henceforth do not allow the disease to break out into a seizure. Thus far concerning the...

...by surgical means. Now to the physical <note>meaning "physic," the practice of internal medicine or natural philosophy</note>:
Take oil of vitriol <term>oil of vitriol: a historical name for concentrated sulfuric acid, used here in a purified alchemical form</term> which has been corrected and aurificated <note>original: "aurificatum"; treated or combined with gold</note>, either tempered through phlegm in a circular fashion, or through the red or green oil which lies hidden beneath^a colcothar: both of these liberate one from the falling sickness <note>original: "morbo caduco"; a traditional term for epilepsy</note>, and this even beyond that age which Hippocrates prescribes <note>Hippocrates famously claimed that if epilepsy did not resolve after puberty, it was generally incurable; the author here claims his alchemical remedies overcome this limitation</note>. Additionally, anointing the back of the head <note>original: "occiput"</note> with balsam of elemi <term>elemi: a fragrant resin obtained from various trees, used in medicinal plasters and ointments</term>, even during the most violent seizure, heals the disease just as well. There are, moreover, other things which contribute not a little to this disease, such as the arcanum <term>arcanum: the "secret" or most potent inner essence of a substance</term> of blood, and other organs. But because some of those are lacking, we shall pass over them, and we shall dwell only on these which are the primary and best.

### On Fevers, Chapter V.

Concerning fevers, hold briefly to this:

### BOOK II ON LONG [DISEASES/LIFE]

Next comes the specific cure and the causal reduction <note>original: *minutio cau-salis*; likely referring to a targeted bloodletting or purging aimed at the underlying cause of the illness</note>, so that *diaceltatesson* <term>diaceltatesson: a complex alchemical remedy involving tartar and minerals, characteristic of the Paracelsian school of medicine</term> precedes the second medicine. For by this purging, every fever residing in the sinning matter <note>original: *materia peccante*; the corrupt or morbid substance believed to be the root of disease in the body</note> is driven out. Furthermore, if the disease does not spread <note>original: *dilatatur*; expand or progress through the body</note>, then neither the specific cure nor the reduction is required. For most often, *diaceltatesson* encompasses the entire cure.

But if it spreads into the limbs, there follows immediately the *opopyron* of Laudanum <term>opopyron Laudani: an alchemical preparation of laudanum (opium), the name suggesting a treatment involving "fire" or high heat</term>, which expels the fever along with the spreading. Now, the reduction—whether of the *salvatella* <term>salvatella: a vein on the back of the hand, traditionally bled to treat the liver or spleen</term>, or the cephalic vein <term>cephalic vein: a major vein in the arm, traditionally bled for ailments of the head</term>, or that reduction which is customarily performed along the spine <note>original: *spinam dorsi*</note>—removes the sinning matter from the blood, specifically that which lies between the skin and the flesh. Therefore, in this instance, there are three things enclosed under a single method of healing <note>original: *medendi rationem*</note>, each perfect in itself.

Finally, so that the physician does not apply more or less than is fitting, he should diligently observe what has spread and what has not spread. I would not wish for anyone here to be deterred by the medical canons <note>the established rules and authoritative texts of the medical establishment</note> of those physicians who disagree with us; for I do not think that matters very much. And how many learned physicians could you find who would die for these canons? <note>A rhetorical challenge suggesting that even the most educated doctors do not truly adhere to the rigid rules of the past when faced with the reality of practice.</note>

...by the surgical method. Now let us turn to the medicinal <note>original: *physicam*; in this context, "physics" refers to the use of natural substances and internal medicines as opposed to manual surgery</note> method:
Take oil of vitriol <term>oil of vitriol: a potent mineral acid, now known as sulfuric acid, which alchemists purified for medicinal use</term> that has been corrected and "goldened" <note>original: *aurificatum*; likely referring to an alchemical process of refining the substance to a "perfect" or "golden" state</note>, or tempered into a circular form by its phlegm <note>original: *phlegma*; the watery distillate produced during the heating of a substance</note>, or by way of the red or green oil which lies hidden beneath <gloss>Colcothar is red calcined vitriol.</gloss> colcothar <term>colcothar: the brownish-red residue left after the distillation of vitriol</term>. Both of these free a person from the falling sickness <note>original: *morbo caduco*; a historical term for epilepsy</note>, even beyond the age that Hippocrates <note>Hippocrates (c. 460 – c. 370 BC) was the ancient Greek physician whose traditional authority Paracelsus frequently challenged</note> prescribes. From this, it can be gathered that Hippocrates, along with his interpreters, did not sufficiently understand those things which drive away epilepsy. Furthermore, if the mercurial part <note>original: *pars mercurialis*; referring to Mercury as one of the three alchemical "Primes"—the volatile or spirituous essence of a substance</note> is anointed with balsam of ivy, specifically after a seizure, it likewise drives out the disease. Additionally, anointing the back of the head <note>original: *occiput*</note> with balsam of elemi <term>balsam of elemi: a fragrant resin harvested from certain trees, used in traditional ointments</term>, even during the most violent seizure, cures the disease just as well. There are, moreover, other things which contribute no small amount to this disease, such as the *arcanum* of blood <term>arcanum: the "secret" or purified spiritual essence of a substance believed to have transformative healing powers</term> and other parts. But because some of those are missing, we shall pass over them and dwell only on these, which are the primary and best.

### Chapter VI: On Apostemes

In the first place, regarding **Apostemes** <term>apostemes: large, inflammatory swellings, boils, or abscesses where the body attempts to expel "corrupt" matter</term>, you will observe that there are two types of manifestations. Some are those which, together with a **paroxysm** <term>paroxysm: a sudden attack or a sharp increase in the severity of a disease's symptoms</term>, bring about death. In these, physicians have labored much while imposing upon men. This wretched and credulous nation of mortals has allowed itself to be deceived by such blatant nonsense <note>original: *nugamentis*; referring to the "trifles" or worthless theories of the medical establishment</note> as has been recorded concerning epidemics and other manifestations of that kind.

Other types are those which bring a paroxysm short of death; these we subject to medicine. Both types meet in **Anthrax** <note>original: *Anthrace*; here referring to a malignant skin lesion or "coal-like" boil, often associated with the plague</note>; similarly in **sapphire** <note>original: *sapphiro*; a blue-tinted pustule or carbuncle</note>, in **carbuncle**, in **pleurisy**, in **plague**, in **bubo**, and many others. In certain cases, no death is inherent: as in **cancer** and other apostemes of that kind which occur in the breasts of women.

In all these cases, the cure follows this principle: aside from **Sonath** <term>Sonath: a specialized Paracelsian remedy, likely a preparation of saffron (Crocus), valued for its ability to "comfort the heart" and expel toxins through the skin</term>, which possesses an expulsive power, receive nothing else. For by the power and force of this remedy, individual open afflictions are driven out. This resolves, mitigates, and finally expels the open [venoms] from the heart. Therefore, surgical practice ought to be present alongside it, with the intervention of **maturatives** <note>medicines used to "ripen" an abscess or bring it to a head</note>, by which in <gloss>a</gloss> the **emunctory** <term>emunctory: any organ or part of the body, such as the armpit or groin, that serves to carry off waste or "corrupt humors"</term>

<meta>The catchword "erdi-" suggests the next page continues with a word like "erdigesta" or "eradicatus," likely describing the completion of the expulsion process.</meta>

<summary>Paracelsus distinguishes between fatal and non-fatal inflammatory swellings, criticizing traditional "epidemic" theories while promoting "Sonath" as a universal expulsive remedy. He emphasizes the need to drive toxins away from the heart toward the body's natural drainage points (emunctories).</summary>

...however, among the **restoratives** <term>restoratives: also called "comfortatives," these were medicines intended to strengthen the organs and restore the body's vital "tone"</term>, you should possess such a remedy. It is made from gold that has not been flattened <note>original: *non planato*; referring to gold that hasn't been beaten into leaf, or perhaps unalloyed</note>, nor mixed with any other unsuitable substance. It is prepared by means of the **athanor** <term>athanor: a specialized alchemical furnace designed to maintain a uniform, constant heat over long periods</term> and the **rubella** <note>original: *rubellam*; likely referring to the "red" stage of the alchemical work or a red-colored elixir</note> with the administration of the red essence. The dose consists of three drops, or according to the physical state of the patient. But the cure from **constrictives** <term>constrictives: astringent medicines used to "bind" the bowels and stop the loss of fluids</term> is as follows: Take one drachm of **oil of Mars** <note>original: *olei martis*; a chemical preparation of iron, usually iron oxide or iron sulfate</note> made from **crocus** <term>crocus: in alchemy, a fine powder usually produced by calcination, here specifically iron oxide (crocus martis)</term>, half a scruple of **laudanum** <term>laudanum: here referring to Paracelsus’s specific compounded medicine, often containing opium and other minerals, used as a powerful sedative and painkiller</term>, one and a half drachms of **theriac** <term>theriac: an ancient "universal" electuary or paste made of many ingredients, used as an antidote and general cure-all</term>, and one and a half scruples of **mummia** <term>mummia: not necessarily a literal mummy, but a Paracelsian term for the "vital balsam" or life-force preserved in organic matter</term>. Let a **bolus** <term>bolus: a large, soft pill</term> be made. Half a drachm is the dose. 

Observe this rule in all "dissolved" conditions: a restorative must be administered. This is because in the early stages and in **cachexia** <term>cachexia: a state of extreme physical wasting and malnutrition associated with chronic disease</term>, the greatest deliberation is required; otherwise, by using only constrictives, the ailment passes into a "second generation" and becomes another disease entirely.

For it drives out worms both downwards and upwards, specifically through the opening of that emunctory. 

<margin>Expulsion of worms</margin>

But if the emunctory is not suitable for egress, or if the place is compressed, kill the worms with **wood sponges** <note>original: *spongijs syluanis*; likely a type of fungal growth or specialized sponge found in the wild</note>, and drive out the putrefied matter using **centaury** <note>a bitter herb frequently used in the Renaissance to treat fevers and digestive issues</note>, or by a **pessary** for the womb, or a **clyster** <term>clyster: an enema; a liquid preparation injected into the lower bowel</term> for the lower intestines, or by mouth for the stomach and upper intestines. 

The best remedy, however, is centaury mixed with the porous substance, with additions according to the medical process from **collodium** <note>likely a gummy or glue-like substance used as a carrier</note>, or with **elecampane** <note>original: *ellenio*; the plant *Inula helenium*, prized for its ability to strengthen the chest and stomach</note> 
->added.<-

LONG DISEASES BOOK II.

### Chapter 10: On Cancer

Furthermore, the **remedy for cancer** is as follows: The first step is the **renewal of the matter**, that is, the **separation of the veins** from impurities. The medicine for this separation is this: Take 1 ounce of **purified althoia** <note>original: *althoia*; a Paracelsian chemical term for a specific mineral preparation</note> of **lead ore**, six ounces of **pellitory juice** <note>original: *parthenionis succi*; a common herb used in traditional medicine for inflammation</note>, and ten ounces of **plantain juice** <note>original: *plantaginis*</note>. Let them be mixed with a small amount of **spirit of wine** <note>original: *alcohol vini*; distilled alcohol</note>. Administer this every six hours. Moisten the affected area <note>original: *periculum*; literally "the danger"</note> with a cloth or a sponge soaked in the **pellitory water** and placed over it. 

Once this is done, after three days, apply **opodeldoc** <term>opodeldoc: a medicinal plaster or liniment, famously popularized by Paracelsus, used for its healing and softening properties</term> composed of prepared simple ingredients and made into a **honeyed wax salve** <note>original: *cerotum mellinum*</note> without the use of **rock alum**. Continue this until the pain becomes mild and the redness subsides. Finally, apply our **common opodeldoc** over the area, leaving it from twelve hours until the next application. 

Furthermore, if a **wanton corrosive ulcer** <note>original: *lasciuuum æſtiomenū*; an "eating-sore" that spreads aggressively</note> should occur, drive it out with the assigned liquids and repeat the process from the center until the ulcer is destroyed. This description of the cure is the **true medicine** for **fistulas** <note>abnormal tube-like passages in the body</note>, cancer arising from **hemorrhoids**, cancer of the **breasts**, severe **inflammation** <note>original: *prauę tentiginis*; referring to a painful tension, hardening, or swelling</note>, and the corruption of **eating-sores**.

### On Syrones <note>original: *Syrones*; mites or small worms that burrow under the skin, often associated with scabies or skin-boring parasites</note> Chapter 11.

#### The cure for Syrones is performed according to this judgment.

<margin>
*Spermiola* <gloss>original: *c*; a reference to frog spawn</gloss> is the spawn of frogs.
*Meloës* <gloss>original: *d*</gloss> are little black beetles <note>original German: *schvartze käferli*</note> found in summer; when dried, they have a sweet scent.
</margin>

Take liquefied frog spawn <gloss>original: *Spermiolæ*; c</gloss> with the mucilage of earthworms [treated] with nitre, and "scaly oil" <term>scaly oil: *oleum squaminum*; likely a mineral oil derived from metal scales, such as copper or iron, used in Paracelsian chemistry</term>. Make from these a sticky paste in a cold place; use it thus: Anoint the place and apply a poultice of it over the area for three days. Then, wipe the area, poultice the skin, and remove the *syrones* <note>the mites</note>. Next, apply the opodeldoc <gloss>original: *f*</gloss> of beetles from sunset until dawn. Once this is done, draw out the disease into a "white bath" <note>original: *balneum albugineum*; likely a medicinal bath made of egg whites or a milky solution</note> as many times as needed until that limb appears white and stable.

This cure is the highest and most perfect for *syrones*, scars, types of *morphea* <note>original: *morphea*; a skin condition causing localized patches of discolored skin</note>, and leprous skin; indeed, it also removes the whole "hoof" <note>original: *vngulam*; likely referring to thickened, horny skin or fungal nail infections</note>, itching, scabies, and the first types of alopecia <note>hair loss</note>, and it drives away the "rosy drop" <note>original: *rosaceam guttam*; now known as rosacea</note>.

# On Syrones Chapter 11.
## The cure for Syrones shall be based on this principle.

<margin>c *Spermiola* is the spawn of frogs.</margin>

LONGA BOOK II.

### *Chapter XII. On Pustules.*

In the various types of the French disease <note>original: *Gallici morbi*; the common historical name for syphilis, which was often characterized by skin eruptions</note>, you shall observe three things: the purging, the cure, and the preservation. Note these few points regarding them. At the start, purge the pustulous patient with *ceniotemium* <term>ceniotemium: a technical term in Paracelsian medicine for a specific purgative preparation, likely involving mineral salts</term>, by which the cause of the disease is driven out along with the offending matter <note>original: *materia peccante*; a medical concept referring to the corrupted bodily fluids believed to cause illness</note>. One must take care, however, not to wander further than is right from the proper dose and quantity of this purgative. Furthermore, if the pustules should cling to the skin on the outside, or if otherwise the pain in the joints has settled deeper into the body, anoint with the oil that is made from realgar <term>realgar: a naturally occurring arsenic sulfide mineral; though toxic, it was frequently used in alchemical medicine for skin ailments</term>, and the sick person will recover. Regarding the ulcers, nodes, and swellings—whether they appear outside or inside the body—which arise from this disease, proceed according to the practical prescriptions regarding ulcers.

### *Chapter XIII. On Ulcers.*

Concerning mortification, take...

...by means of the alkali which is made from realgar <term>realgar: a toxic arsenic sulfide mineral; Paracelsus often used it in treated forms for skin conditions</term>. With this, you must mortify <note>original: *mortifica*; in early medicine, this meant to kill off diseased or "dead" tissue so that the body could begin to heal</note> whatever in the body is corrupted or defective. Nevertheless, all these things, however they may be, we leave to the judgment of the surgeon. There are, moreover, countless other receipts used by physicians; since they have deceived people more than once, and those who devised them perished by their own inventions, I see no reason why you should use them. This much regarding ulcers. The purging, which by right ought to be the first step for the preservation of the body, I entrust to the prudent physician according to the instructions we previously prescribed regarding pustules.

**T**here is a threefold method for healing wounds. The first is Natural, such as with balsams; the Spe[cific]...

...specifics, such as *brassidellia* <term>brassidellia: a specific herbal or alchemical preparation mentioned by Paracelsus for wound healing</term>, and characteristic remedies, such as *magorreo* <term>magorreo: a Paracelsian term for remedies that act through an essential or "magical" internal power</term>. Before we come to those, however, we will first point out things that will seem like a miracle to those who are unskilled in these matters: namely, that neither the type of wound nor its location on the body needs to be a point of obsession. This is a subject the ancient doctors discussed at quite tedious length: debating whether wounds of the brain, the intestines, or the bladder could be healed at all. They argued this because we see that wounds—whether they are deep stabs or inflicted in some other way—result in death when they occur in a place where life cannot be sustained. 

Therefore, we will say nothing at all about fatal wounds for the time being, as they reveal themselves for what they are. Furthermore, the first caution is this: that the surgeon must not take a wound that is healable and, through his own actions, make it unhealable or even fatal. This, therefore, is the task, this is the labor <note>original: *Hoc opus, hic labor est*; a famous reference to Virgil's Aeneid, implying a difficult but essential challenge</note>—that you rightly recognize this threefold method of healing wounds, which is by far the most healthful. In the meantime, do not listen to those who go about asking for I know not what vain inventions—indeed, seeking them even with prayers from low-ranking barber-surgeons <term>barber-surgeons: practitioners who performed surgery and hair-cutting, often dismissed by academic physicians as unlearned</term>, servants, and other such frivolous men...

...tend, among whom are Lanfranc <term>Lanfranc of Milan (c. 1250–1306): a highly influential medieval surgeon whose "Chirurgia Magna" was a standard text</term> and Peter of Argillata <term>Pietro d’Argelata (d. 1423): an Italian surgeon known for his commentaries on Avicenna</term>, who have piled up many and various things—not worth a nutshell <note>original: *nuce non æstimanda*; a Latin idiom meaning something is of no value</note>—concerning wounds into a single volume. O, truly hatted little doctors! You who, besides your reddish little caps and your titles, wear nothing else noteworthy; you who seek a flooding Nile from a pumice stone <note>original: *ex pumice Nilum exundantē petitis*; a variation on a proverb about seeking water from a stone, implying these doctors look for great wisdom in dry, useless sources</note>. The blind follows the blind, and the old follows the old.

This is the reason why those who have followed the School of Montpellier <note>original: *Montem Pessulanum*; Montpellier was one of the most famous medical universities in medieval Europe, which Paracelsus frequently criticized for its rigid adherence to ancient tradition</note> have turned this threefold medicine of wounds to a foreign use. Specifically, they have preposterously turned the **natural** medicine, which belongs to balsam <term>balsam: in Paracelsian medicine, this refers to the "internal balsam" or "mumia," the body's innate power to heal itself, which can be supported by certain herbs</term>, into a cause of putrefaction. They have twisted the **specific** remedy toward those parts of the body where it least belongs, and not without danger. As for the **characteristics**, these have been so abused by traveling quacks <note>original: *circunforanei*; literally "those who hang around the marketplace," referring to unlicensed street-surgeons or peddlers</note> that they have fallen into utter contempt.

We, however, shall openly hand down what is truly held regarding these matters in the following opinion. **Natural balsam**, when administered as a potion or otherwise poured into wounds, is nothing other than *hypericum* <gloss>St. John's wort</gloss>, *centaurea* <gloss>centaury</gloss>, and *prunella* <gloss>self-heal</gloss>. These herbs heal not only those wounds that are on the outside of the body, but also those that are internal, and this is because of the power that exists in the balsam. Whatever pertains to the **specific**, however, exceeds the nature of balsam by many degrees. For *collatenna* <note>likely *Sanicula europaea* or Sanicle, an herb famous for "knitting" wounds together</note>, when administered as a potion...

# LONGA LIB. II. 53

...administered, it heals both external and internal wounds, because of a virtue <note>original: *virtutem*; in this context, it refers to a specific healing power or "inner essence" of a substance</note> that is so firm and so fixed within it; it continues to work as long as the wound is open. **Brassatella** <term>Brassatella: likely the herb Sanicle (*Sanicula europaea*), highly regarded in folk medicine for its ability to "glue" wounds shut</term>, if it merely touches the wound, requires no further medicine. 

There are, moreover, many other **specifics** <note>specifics: remedies that have a unique, "specific" power to target a particular ailment without needing complex compounding</note> which work in the same way—and not only for twelve or twenty-three hours. Their power is so great that they provide a fixed and indeed perpetual remedy, even if used only once, and that right up to the end of the wound's healing. In this place, specific experiments should have been listed, but because we have made mention of them in the *Archidoxis* <note>original: *Archidoxa*; one of Paracelsus's most famous works, "The Archidoxes of Theophrastus," which details the "mysteries" of nature and chemical medicine</note>, I think it best to pass over them.

Concerning **characters** <note>original: *characteribus*; mystical symbols, sigils, or celestial signs used in Hermetic medicine to channel spiritual healing</note>, which is the third form of medicine, let it be understood thus: Characters, blessings, and other things of that kind—whether the healer is present at the wound or absent, or if they are applied directly—heal individual wounds, and they do so without any *synonymia* <term>synonymia: a Paracelsian term likely referring to a type of morbid discharge, "mummy," or complication in the healing process</term>.

There are also those things that stop "white synonymia," such as **staraphaxat** <term>staraphaxat: a technical term in Paracelsian surgery for a remedy (often magical or alchemical) that stops a white discharge or flux</term>; others stop blood, such as **kist** and **kiss** <term>kist and kiss: Paracelsian jargon for remedies or words of power used to staunch bleeding (styptics)</term>, and countless others of that kind which are listed elsewhere in the *Archidoxis*. These things alone are what ennoble the physician. Therefore, in this threefold method of healing wounds, a general care should be applied according to what is prescribed; meanwhile, one should not listen to those who have imposed their deceptions upon the whole world.

These herbs heal wounds not only on the outside but also on the inside, and this is because of the power <note>original: *virtutem*; referring to the "virtue" or essential healing property of the plant.</note> that exists within the balsam.

For **Collatenna** <note>original: *collatenna*; likely referring to a "vulnerary" herb like Sanicle or Ironwort, believed to "collate" or glue the edges of a wound together.</note>, when administered as a potion...

<header>THE LONG SURGERY, BOOK II.</header>

[Admin]istered, it heals both external and internal wounds, on account of the virtue <note>original: *virtutem*; in this context, it refers to the inherent medicinal power or "quintessence" of a substance.</note> which is usually so firm and so fixed within it, and it works for as long as the wound is open. **Brassatella** <note>original: *Brassatella*; likely refers to Adder's Tongue (*Ophioglossum vulgatum*), a herb Paracelsus highly valued for wound healing.</note>, if it merely touches the wound, requires no further medicine. There are, moreover, many other **Specifics** which work in the same manner: and not just for twelve or twenty-three hours. The power of these is such that they provide a fixed and perpetual medicine, even if used only once, and this continues until the very end of the wound’s healing. Specific experiments were to be reviewed in this place, but because we have already made mention of them in the *Archidoxis* <note>The *Archidoxis* (or *Archidoxes of Theophrastus*) is one of Paracelsus's most famous works on the "ancient" or "original" doctrines of alchemy and medicine.</note>, I judge that they should be passed over.

Regarding **Characters** <note>original: *characteribus*; mystical symbols, sigils, or spoken charms used in "celestial" or sympathetic medicine.</note>, which is the third form, understand it thus: Characters, blessings, and others of that kind, whether the patient is present or absent, when applied, heal individual wounds, and they do so without any <term>synoniam</term> <note>original: *synoniam*; in Paracelsian medicine, this often refers to a discharge, joint fluid, or a specific type of "mortal" humor that hinders healing.</note>. There are even those which stop the "white" <term>synoniam</term>, such as **staraphaxat** <note>A Paracelsian term for a specific balsamic substance or alchemical preparation used to halt discharges.</note>; others stop the blood, such as **kist** and **kiss** <note>Esoteric terms used by Paracelsus for remedies—often minerals or balsams—that staunch bleeding.</note>, and countless others of that kind which are reviewed elsewhere in the *Archidoxis*. These are the things—and these alone—which ennoble a physician. Therefore, in this threefold method of healing wounds, general care must be applied according to what has been prescribed; meanwhile, one must not listen to those who <gloss>who</gloss> have deceived the entire world.

<summary>Paracelsus explains that "Specific" herbs and "Characters" (mystical symbols) provide a permanent healing power that does not require constant re-application, unlike traditional Galenic ointments. He references his own book, the Archidoxis, for further detail on these esoteric substances like staraphaxat and kist.</summary>

# ON LONG LIFE,
## Book Three.

### CHAPTER I.

<image-desc>A large historiated woodcut initial 'P' depicting a seated figure, likely a scholar or physician, gesturing with their hands within a frame of foliage and architectural motifs.</image-desc>

After we have spoken of the specific secret remedies <term>arcanis: hidden medicinal powers or "mysteries" extracted from nature</term> which restore a body worn down by disease to its former health, we shall begin from the point where the previous sections ended. In order that those things handed down in the earlier books may finally be completed by us, and the physical body might be preserved like a balm <term>balsamum: a preservative substance, often compared to the resins used in ancient times to prevent decay</term>—and even though the specific secrets and the subject matter of this third book refer to the same body, and furthermore, one and the same preparation exists—nonetheless, the practice concerning this third elixir <term>elixir: a potent alchemical preparation intended to prolong life or transform substances</term> is different from that singular method of healing. 

Therefore, in this third book, the "flower of cheyri" <note>original: "flos cheyri," referring to the wallflower, valued in alchemy for its restorative properties</note> and "anthos" <note>original: "anthos," the Greek word for "flower," often specifically referring to rosemary flowers in early modern pharmacology</note> hold the primary place. For in this book, the secret of elixirs is completed, and that very thing is achieved by the power and indeed the virtue of the entire quintessence <term>quinta essentia: the "fifth essence," the purest and most potent spiritual core of a substance, beyond the four physical elements</term>. First, therefore, so that each detail may become more clearly known, I will briefly indicate what the quintessence is, according to this meaning. Which...

<margin>What the flower of gold is</margin>
Moreover, the flower of gold, the flower of amethyst, and finally whatever is of a transparent nature—of pearls, of sulfurous substances, of **cachymiae** <term>cachymia: an unrefined ore or the "scum" of metals, often considered a "half-metal" by Paracelsians</term>, and lastly also whatever is of the alum-like **zerebothina** <term>zerebothinum: a specialized Paracelsian term for certain metallic salts or vitriols used in chemical preparation</term>, and other things of that kind which water produces, such as **amber** <term>carabara: from "carabe," a term for amber or resin</term> and corals. All these things, I say, are subject to the quintessence, according to the method of tempering which usually occurs through the intervention of the "corruption" <note>In alchemy, "corruption" is the necessary breakdown or decay of a substance before it can be reborn or purified into a higher form</note> of the elements by the spagyrist.

ᵃ *A spagyrist is one who understands the natures of things not by authority, but by his own experience.*

ON LONG LIFE, BOOK III.

### CHAPTER II.

Furthermore, the sum of this whole matter lies in this (since each of those things said in this book concerning the **elixir** <note>original: "elixiro," a medicinal substance believed to prolong life or transform matter</note> are to be related to long life): that the whole of nature is reduced into a **spagyric mixture** <term>spagyric mixture: a balanced compound created through the Paracelsian method of "separating and recombining" elements to reach a purified state</term>, or a tempering, which is nothing other than the goodness of nature, in which there is nothing corruptible and, finally, nothing adverse. And although that same goodness of nature is perceived in different ways and through various means in a **tincture** <note>original: "tinctura," a potent medicinal extract</note> according to the prescription of nature—which exists in the **stone of the physicists** <note>original: "lapide physicorum," commonly known as the Philosopher's Stone</note>; in **antimony** <note>original: "antimonio"</note> according to the **nature of the raven** <note>original: "corvi naturam," a reference to the black state of matter or the heavy, dark appearance of antimony ore</note>; in sulfur according to the effect of **Lunaria** <note>original: "Lunariæ," a plant often called moonwort, associated with lunar properties</note>, and in others of that kind—

<margin><unclear>⟋ * mm io</unclear></margin>

Nevertheless, in all of these, there is one and the same tempering, which lies hidden among metals under **Mercury** <note>original: "Mercurio"; the author refers to the philosophical principle of volatility present in all metals, not just liquid quicksilver</note>: (I say Mercury, which is in all metals); among gems, under **crystal**; among stones, under **zeloto** <term>zelotus: an archaic or specialized term for a specific type of crystalline stone</term>; among liquors, under **carabe** <term>carabe: a medieval term for amber, derived from Arabic</term>; among herbs, under **valerian**; among roots, under **peucedanum** <term>peucedanum: hog's fennel, a medicinal root used in ancient and medieval pharmacy</term>; among bitter things, under **vitriol** <term>vitriol: a sulfate mineral, often associated with the "bitterness" or acidic core of substances</term>; among ferns, under **antimony**. Furthermore, just as mercury is present in each individual metal, so is antimony in ferns, vitriol in salt, and **melissa** <note>original: "melissa," lemon balm</note> in herbs. These are the names...

...of a tempered **elixir**. It must therefore be observed in the following elixirs that their **sulfur**—which some call minerals—is actually the **quintessence** <term>quintessence: the "fifth essence" or the purest, most concentrated form of a substance, believed by alchemists to be the medicinal core of a body</term>, the **mercury of metals** <note>original: "mercurium metallorum"</note>, from which the very nature of the body is drawn out. For **cheyri** <note>original: "cheyri," an alchemical term often referring to the floral essence of gold or certain yellow plants like wallflowers</note> belongs to **Venus** <note>In alchemical symbolism, Venus represents the metal copper</note>, and **anthos** <note>original: "anthos," Greek for "flower"</note> to **Mars** <note>In alchemical symbolism, Mars represents the metal iron</note>. Their power and nature is not so much to drive away diseases, but rather to preserve that body for a long life—a life that is otherwise subject to the influences of the lower world.

In this regard, we wish for the elixirs of long life to be understood by many and various names, since their power is one and the same. As for us (if perhaps you wonder at this method of treatment), it has seemed appropriate for the time being to play with these terms.

### CHAPTER III.

The remaining elixirs, which are not separated from the body, will be indicated below where we make mention of **wine**. Regarding the elixir of gold, then, as it pertains to **practice** <note>original: "praxin," referring to the laboratory work or actual application of the theory</note>, proceed as follows: Dissolve gol-

# ON LONG LIFE BOOK III.

...together with the whole substance of gold in a **corrosive** <term>corrosive: an acidic substance, often called "strong water," used to dissolve metals into a liquid state</term>, and let it remain there just as long as it becomes one with the corrosive itself. Nor, in the meantime, should the mind shrink from this method of treatment; for gold, insofar as it is gold, excels in the corrosive, and without the corrosive it is dead. Therefore, we assert that the **quintessence** of gold is useless without the corrosive.

It follows, then, that the dissolved gold must be removed once again through **putrefaction** <term>putrefaction: in alchemy, a process of breakdown or "decay" through gentle heat, allowing a substance to be reborn in a purer state</term>, even if the corrosive adheres quite stubbornly. For if the power of gold is so great that it preserves the body, renders it free from every illness, and does not allow it to be corrupted, how much more does it preserve itself—and that without any infection? For it corrects and mends whatever is not pure.

Therefore, the corrosive within the gold should by no means be called a "corrosive": for the power of the **arcanum** <term>arcanum: the secret, essential healing principle of a substance that acts directly on the human life force</term> overcomes every poison. For all **realgar** <note>original: "realgar," a toxic arsenic sulfide mineral; here used to represent all poisonous or corruptive qualities</note> dies <gloss>original: "moritur"</gloss> in the **elixir** of gold, and passes away into an excellent medicinal tincture.

And indeed, in this manner, **potable gold** <note>original: "aurum potabile," literally "drinkable gold," the ultimate alchemical medicine believed to grant longevity</note> is produced after putrefaction. The common practice of the **spagyrists** <term>spagyrists: physicians and alchemists who used the "spagyric" method of separating and recombining natural elements to create pure medicines</term> prescribes this dose, or if you prefer, this certain "harmony." Finally, you shall observe concerning the elixir—

->Chapter IV: On Pearls<-

But so that those things which we have said concerning the **quintessence** <term>quintessence: the "fifth essence," the purest spiritual core of a material, separated from the four heavy elements</term> may shine forth more clearly, it must be observed that nothing is more closely related to gold than pearls. Therefore, you must bring the four elements found within pearls into a tempered balance with gold in the same manner; this is what truly exists as the quintessence, achieved without the loss of the underlying substances.

Furthermore, if you wish to transmute pearls into the quintessence according to the prescription, proceed according to the logic of the quintessence. Change nothing except the starting point, in which it is necessary to join the **ultimate matter** <note>original: "ultimam materiam," the final, perfected state of a substance</note> which serves as the **prime matter** <term>prime matter: the "first material" or raw starting base required to begin an alchemical work</term> in the preparation of the quintessence of the **Sun** <note>original: "solis," the alchemical name for gold</note>.

This is drawn out from a recipe of this kind: First, reduce **lemon** <note>original: "limonem"; likely referring to lemon juice or a similar organic acid used as a solvent to break down the calcium of the pearls</note> into a liquid, then set it aside; within this, the pearls undergo **calcination** <term>calcination: the process of heating a substance or treating it with acid to reduce it to a fine, purified powder</term>. Once dried, they are dissolved, and this dissolution breaks forth into an element in which absolutely no physical **complexion** <term>complexion: the specific balance of humors or qualities in a physical body; here meaning the pearls have reached a state of pure, non-physical simplicity</term> remains. For the entire power of this substance is similar to the quintessence. I would not wish for you to admit in this place the reason...

...the method of extraction which **Archelaus** <note>original: "Archelaus"; an ancient philosopher often cited in alchemical traditions as an authority on the origins of matter</note> prescribes, nor other **spagyric** <term>spagyric: the alchemical process of separating a substance into its elemental parts and recombining them in a purified form</term> separations of that kind. For the superior transmutation not only restores those limbs that are weak to their former health, but also preserves those which are strong and robust in that same vigor. This is why **pearls** are valued more highly than other "seeds" <note>original: "spermata"; in this context, it refers to the foundational essences or reproductive principles of natural things</note>; among which I affirm those of **oysters** to be by far the most excellent.

Although in this place it plays no small part, just as the ***Archidoxa*** <note>original: "Archidoxa"; referring to Paracelsus's own work, *The Archidoxes of Theophrastus*, which deals with the highest mysteries of nature</note> holds. The **Homunculus** <term>homunculus: a "little man"; an artificially created human being produced in a laboratory through alchemical fermentation</term>, which necromancers commonly call ***Alreona*** and natural philosophers commonly call the **mandrake** <note>original: "mandragoram"</note>: yet it has fallen into common error because of that "chaos" with which those people have obscured the true use of the homunculus. Its origin is indeed from seed: for through the greatest **digestion** <note>original: "digestionem"; in alchemy, this refers to the application of gentle, steady heat over a long period to mature a substance</note> which occurs in a **horse's belly** <term>horse's belly: "venter equinus"; an alchemical term for a digestion vat kept warm by being buried in fermenting horse manure, which provides a constant, natural heat</term>, a **homunculus** is generated, similar in all respects—in body and blood, in primary and secondary limbs—to that from which it originated. Therefore, we will pass over its virtues in this place, since it has not become as well known among experts in this matter as pearls have.

THEOPHRASTUS ON LIFE
### On the Extraction of the quintessence from herbs.
->Chapter V.<-

Earlier we made mention of the **quintessence** <term>quintessence: the "fifth element," a pure, medicinal essence thought to be hidden within the four physical elements</term>, which ought to be made without any extraction; you must understand this in the following way. In the **Elixir** <note>original: "Elixiro"; a preparation intended to prolong life or change the nature of a substance</note>, which we are mentioning at present, the quintessence can in no way be drawn out from herbs without extraction, because of the diversity of essences that exist within a single substance. These must be so separated that the herb remains herb, and the quintessence remains quintessence. Although in any herb there are the four elements doubled <note>the theory that elements have dual qualities, such as "hot and dry" or "cold and moist"</note>: nevertheless the quintessence is not present in a doubled form, but only as one part.

We leave the other part, which belongs to the material substance, to those arts which are specific, and we shall treat that which belongs to the elixir: which will become clearer through the example of **lemon balm** <note>original: "melissæ"</note>. Digest the lemon balm for a philosophical month <note>typically 40 days, the time required for a complete alchemical cycle</note> in <gloss>a</gloss> **athanor** <term>athanor: a furnace designed to maintain a constant, uniform heat over long periods</term>, then separate it in such a way that the doubled elements appear separately; immediately the quintessence will shine forth, which is the **elixir of life**. Similarly in noble wine, and the same varies: in bitter **catmint** <note>original: "nepita"</note> [it appears differently], in **darnel** <note>original: "lollio"</note> it is yellow: in **Tincium** <note>likely referring to the Tench fish or a specific mineral preparation</note>, it is blackish.

## On the Extraction of the quintessence from herbs.
### Chapter V.

<margin>
What the alchemical matter is, and how the quintessence is extracted; regarding the quintessence, see folio 102, 4 quintis <unclear>11. h. 4.</unclear>

Above, we made mention of the quintessence <note>original: "quintæ essentiæ"; in alchemy, the "fifth essence" is the purest concentrated medicinal force of a plant, beyond the four physical elements</note> which should be formed without any [traditional] extraction; you ought to understand this in the following sense. In the Elixir, which we are mentioning at present, the quintessence cannot by any means be drawn out from herbs without extraction, because of the diversity of essences that exist within a single substance. These things are to be separated in such a way that the herb remains an herb, and the quintessence remains the quintessence. Although in any given herb there are four **doubled elements** <note>original: "elementa duplata"; Paracelsus often viewed the elements as having a dual nature—one physical/impure and one spiritual/pure</note>, the quintessence is not present in a doubled form, but as one part alone. The other part, which belongs to the substance, we leave to those arts which are specialized, and we shall treat that which pertains to the elixir: this will become clearer through the example of **lemon balm** <note>original: "melissa"; a fragrant herb long used in alchemy and medicine for its soothing and life-extending properties</note>.

Digest the lemon balm for a **philosophical month** <note>a "philosophical month" usually refers to a period of 40 days, the time required for a complete chemical transformation</note> in an **athanor** <term>athanor: a furnace used by alchemists designed to maintain a steady, uniform heat over long periods</term>, then separate it so that the doubled elements appear separately; immediately the quintessence will shine forth, which is the elixir of life. Similarly in noble wine, and likewise in various ways: in bitter **catnip** <note>original: "nepita"</note>; in **darnel** <note>original: "lollio"; a type of grass often considered a weed but studied for its potent chemical properties</note>, it is yellow; in **tincion** <note>original: "Tincio"; likely referring to a substance or plant used for dyeing or staining</note>, it is blackening:

...the virtue of **antimony** <note>original: "antimonium"; a metallic element that Paracelsus considered one of the most potent, though dangerous, medicinal substances</note>. <margin>It cleanses itself.</margin>

## On Sulfur Chapter VII.

To begin with, it is a difficult matter and one to be celebrated by many names, that there is such great power and nature in the earth which produces a **balsam** <term>balsamum; a preservative substance or vital essence believed to prevent decay and maintain health</term>, the nature of which is such that it allows nothing further to rot.
